generates its first response only based on
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
, and thus changing the value of
\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}
from zero to the arbitrary constant
0.1
is unimportant.

Upon receiving a left message of the form
((q,j),(q^{\prime},j))
, the IPM
\mathcal{P}
sends
(q,q^{\prime})
as a left message to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
. Upon receipt,
\mathcal{T}_{j}
may interact with its right mechanism. As described below,
\mathcal{P}
simulates the interaction of
\mathcal{T}_{j}
with its right mechanism until
\mathcal{T}_{j}
sends a left message
m
.
\mathcal{P}
then returns
m
as a response to its left mechanism. By Corollary
5.7
,
\mathcal{T}_{j}
sends at most two right messages.

When
\mathcal{T}_{j}
sends a right message for the first time (i.e., when
w_{j}=\text{null}
),
\mathcal{P}
requests the creation of
\mathrm{RR}_{0,\delta_{j}^{\prime}}
by sending the message
(\beta_{0,\delta_{j}^{\prime}},\beta_{0,\delta_{j}^{\prime}})
to its right mechanism
\mathcal{V}[f^{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
.
\mathcal{P}
discards the acknowledgment response
\top
, increments
n
by one, and sends the message
\left((0,n),(1,n)\right)
to its right mechanism. We will later show that
\mathcal{V}[f^{\mathrm{RR}}]
never halts the communication. Given the initial state
b\in\{0,1\}
, upon receiving
\left((0,n),(1,n)\right)
, the identifier
\mathcal{I}(b)
forwards
(b,n)
to
ExtConComp
, which in turn sends
b
to
\mathrm{RR}_{0,\delta_{j}^{\prime}}
. The response of
\mathrm{RR}_{0,\delta_{j}^{\prime}}
is then forwarded to
\mathcal{P}
unchanged. Let
(\tau_{j},z_{j})\in\{\top,\bot\}\times\{0,1\}
denote this response.
\mathcal{P}
sets
w_{j}=(\tau_{j},z_{j})
and gives
\tau
as a right response to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
. Note that if
\tau_{j}=\bot
, the bit
b
is exposed and
z_{j}=b
.

When
\mathcal{T}_{j}
sends a second right message (i.e., when
w_{j}\neq\text{null}
),
\mathcal{P}
proceeds as follows:

•

If
\tau_{j}=\bot
, then
\mathcal{P}
directly returns
z_{j}
as the right response to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
, thereby revealing the initial state of
\mathcal{I}
.

•

Otherwise,
\mathcal{P}
requests the creation of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},0}
by sending
(\beta_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},0},\beta_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},0})
to its right mechanism
\mathcal{V}[f^{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
. As before,
\mathcal{P}
drops the acknowledgment response
\top
, increments the counter
n
, and sends
\left((0,n),(1,n)\right)
to its right mechanism. In this case,
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},0}
receives the initial state of
\mathcal{I}
as input and returns a response
(\tau_{j}^{\prime},z_{j}^{\prime})
, which is forwarded unchanged to
\mathcal{P}
. By definition
\tau_{j}^{\prime}=\top
with probability
1
. Finally,
\mathcal{P}
sends
z_{j}^{\prime}
as a right response to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
.

By the definition of
f
, there are at most
k
mechanisms among the creation queries sent by
\mathcal{V}[f]
that receive messages of the form
((q,j),(q^{\prime},j))
with
q\neq q^{\prime}
, and the parameter
\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}
of these mechanisms form a sub-multiset of
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
. By Corollary
5.7
, only the IPM
\mathcal{T}_{j}
corresponding to these mechanisms may send a second message to their right mechanisms. Moreover, by the definition of
f
, the parameters
\delta_{j}^{\prime}
of all mechanisms satisfy
1-\prod_{j}(1-\delta_{j}^{\prime})\delta^{\prime}
. Thus, by the design of
\mathcal{P}
, the verifier
\mathcal{V}[f^{\mathrm{RR}}]
never halts the communication and forwards all messages to the opposite side unchanged.

Comparing Definition
2.9
and Definition
2.3
, we observe that the left responses generated by each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
are identically distributed to those that would be produced if it were interacting directly with the IM
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime}}(b)
. Thus, by Corollary
5.7
, each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
simulates
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
identically, implying that the IMs
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{V}[f^{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are equivalent.
∎

5.3.
Composition Theorem for Restricted Verification Functions.

In Theorem
5.2
, we showed that for any positive
\delta
, the concurrent
1
-sparse parallel composition of CMs with the privacy parameter
(0,\delta)
fails to preserve privacy. In our counterexample, the adversary leveraged the first outcome of a
(0,\delta)
-DP CM to decide whether to spend its budget on issuing non-identical queries to that mechanism. Intuitively, when
\delta>0
, there might exist certain “bad first answers” that occur with low probability; however, once we condition on their occurrences, the distribution of the second answer ceases to be differentially private.

In this section, we force the adversary to decide whether it will always issue pairs of identical queries to a CM when it sends the first message to that mechanism. Specifically, if the first message sent to a CM includes identical queries, then all subsequent messages for that CM must also have identical queries. This leads to the following definition.

Definition 5.12 (First-Pair Consistent).

A verification function
f
is
first-pair consistent
if the following holds: for every input message sequence
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
,
f(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})=\bot
whenever
m_{1}
is a pair of identical messages, i.e.,
m_{1}=(m^{\prime},m^{\prime})
for some
m^{\prime}
, and there exists
2\leq j\leq t
such that
m_{j}
is not a pair of identical messages.

For instance, let
\mathcal{M}
be an IM that is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
. As discussed in Section
3.1
,
\mathcal{M}
can be represented by a CM
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(with initial state
null
) that receives an initial state
s\in S_{\mathcal{M}}^{\text{init}}
as the first message and behaves the same as
\mathcal{M}(s)
afterwards. Define the verification function
f_{\sim}
as follows: given an input message sequence
(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})
,
f_{\sim}
returns
\top
if and only if
m_{1}
is a pair of neighboring initial states in
S_{\mathcal{M}}^{\text{init}}
, and for all
2\leq j\leq t
,
m_{j}
is a pair of identical queries in
Q_{\mathcal{M}}
. By definition,
f_{\sim}
is first-pair consistent.

To formally define the restricted variant of the concurrent parallel composition of CMs, we introduce the verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
, where
FPC
stands for first-pair consistency.

Definition 5.13 (
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
).

For any
t\in\mathbb{N}
and any input message sequence
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
, the function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
returns
\top
if and only if both of the following conditions hold:

(i)

f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty}(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})=\top

(ii)

For every message
m_{j}
of the form
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},s^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime},f^{\prime})^{2}
, the verification function
f^{\prime}
is first-pair consistent.

We refer to the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the
FPC concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of CMs with privacy parameters
\left((\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{k}
.

Theorem 5.14.

For
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}>0
,
\epsilon\geq 0
and
0\leq\delta,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k}\leq 1
, if the composition of non-interactive mechanisms
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then the FPC concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of CMs with the privacy parameters
(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
is also
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP against an adaptive adversary.

Proof.

We denote
f
as shorthand for
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
. The proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
4.12
, except for the definition of IPMs
\mathcal{T}_{j}
that are executed by the IPM
\mathcal{P}
: In the proof of Theorem
4.12
, each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
was defined according to Lemma
4.7
. In this proof, each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is defined by Corollary
5.7
if the first message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
satisfies
q_{0}\neq q_{1}
, and it behaves identically to
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
otherwise.

To implement this, the sequence
\sigma_{2}
in
\mathcal{P}
’s state must include an additional boolean variable
u_{j}
for each created mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
, indicating whether the mechanism has previously received any messages. Specifically, when
\mathcal{P}
receives the creation query pair
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
from the verifier, instead of adding
(\mathcal{T}_{j},t_{j},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})
, it adds the tuple
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},u_{j}=0)
to
\sigma_{2}
.

Upon receiving a left message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
, if
u_{j}=0
, before responding,
\mathcal{P}
replaces the
j
-th entry
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},u_{j}=0)
in
\sigma_{2}
with
(\mathcal{T}_{j},t_{j},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},1)
, where
T_{j}
is an IPM defined as follows and
t_{j}
is its initial state:

•

If
q_{0}=q_{1}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is an IPM with the single initial state
\left((),0,s_{j}^{\prime}\right)
that never interacts with its right mechanism and interacts with its left mechanism exactly as
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
. That is, upon receiving a right message,
\mathcal{T}_{j}
halts the communication, and upon receiving a left message, it updates its state and responds identically to
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
.

•

If
q_{0}\neq q_{1}
, then
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is the IPM defined by Lemma
4.7
corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
.

To generate a response to a left message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
, as before,
\mathcal{P}
forwards
(q_{0},q_{1})
to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
as a left message and provide a properly chosen
r_{i}
as a right response if needed (see the proof of Theorem
4.12
).

By Definition
5.13
(ii), we know that for each index
j
, if
q_{0}=q_{1}
in the first message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
, then
q_{0}=q_{1}
in all subsequent messages of this form. In this case, by the above definition, for each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the mechanism
\mathcal{T}_{j}
never interacts with
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime}}(b)
and simulates
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
identically. Moreover, when
q_{0}\neq q_{1}
in the first message, by Definition
5.13
(i) and Definition
5.1
(ii), the privacy parameters
(\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})
corresponding to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
s with the second definition form a sub-multiset of
(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
. Therefore, whenever an IPM
\mathcal{T}_{j}
requires a sample from
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{P}
can find such sample in
\sigma_{1}
that is unused.
∎

5.3.1.
Concurrent
k
-sparse Parallel Composition of IM

Consider a setting where an adversary creates (an unlimited number of) IMs over time while adaptively asking queries from the existing ones. When creating an IM
\mathcal{M}_{i}
that is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
, the adversary also selects a pair of neighboring initial states for
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. This selection depends on the history of queries asked so far and their responses. Based on a secret bit
b
, either all created mechanisms are initialized with the first initial state in their respective pairs, or all are initialized with the second. The goal of the adversary is to distinguish whether
b=0
or
b=1
.

This scenario is an special case of FPC concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of CMs, where, instead of creating
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and assigning a pair of initial states to it, the adversary sends
(\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime},\text{null},\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i},f_{\sim_{i}})^{2}
, where
\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}
is the CM simulating
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. Moreover, to ask a query
q
from the
i
-th created IM, the adversary sends
\left((q,i),(q,i)\right)
. Since each
f_{\sim_{i}}
is first-pair consistent, Theorem
5.14
applies to the
concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of IM
(that are differentially private w.r.t. neighbor relations).

Corollary 5.15.

For
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}>0
,
\epsilon\geq 0
and
0\leq\delta,\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{k}\leq 1
, if the composition of non-interactive mechanisms
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then the concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of IM with privacy parameters
\left((\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})\right)_{i=1}^{k}
is also
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.

NIMs are a special subclass of IMs that halt the communication upon receiving a second input message. Since Theorem
5.14
applies to all IMs, it also holds for the
concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of NIMs with privacy parameters
\left((\epsilon_{1},\delta_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{k}
.
In the next section, we compare the
k
-sparse parallel composition of IMs to the classical parallel composition theorem for NIMs.

5.3.2.
Comparison with the Parallel Composition of NIMs.

Consider a set of
\ell
NIMs,
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
, where each
\mathcal{N}_{i}
has a domain
\mathcal{X}_{i}
and is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
. The
k
-sparse parallel composition of NIMs

\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{\ell}
is said to be
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP if the non-interactive mechanism
{\textrm{Comp}}(\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{\ell})
with the domain
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{X}_{\ell}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim_{\text{par}}
, defined as follows: for any two
x=(x_{1},\dots x_{\ell})
and
x^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots x_{\ell}^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{X}
, we have
x\sim_{\text{par}}x^{\prime}
if and only if there exists a subset
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\{1,\dots,\ell\}
of size at most
k
such that
x_{j}\sim_{j}x_{j}^{\prime}
for every
j\in\mathcal{J}
, and
x_{j}=x_{j}^{\prime}
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}\setminus\mathcal{J}
.

The concurrent parallel composition of IMs generalizes the parallel composition of NIMs in several key ways: Rather than composing a fixed set of
\ell
NIMs, an adversary adaptively selects an
unbounded number
of IMs with arbitrary privacy parameters. Additionally, instead of non-adaptively selecting worst-case neighboring initial states for (at most)
k
mechanisms and identical initial states for the other
\ell-k
mechanisms, the adversary adaptively (1) chooses pairs of initial states based on prior interactions with other mechanisms, while (2) ensuring that the initial states in at most
k
pairs are neighboring, and that they are identical in the other pairs. In the concurrent parallel composition of CMs, the adversary is even stronger: instead of choosing a single pair of neighboring initial states,
it adaptively selects a sequence of pairs
, satisfying the constraints enforced by a verification function.

The
1
-sparse parallel composition of NIMs is commonly used when a dataset is partitioned among
\ell
NIMs such that for any two neighboring datasets differing by a single record, all partitions remain identical except for the presence of the differing record in one partition. Consider the case where an adversary creates CMs receiving data or empty records as query messages. In the concurrent parallel composition of CMs, the adversary adaptively determines the number of partitions, assigns records to them, and selects which partitions contain differing sets of records.

It is known that the
k
-sparse parallel composition of
\ell

(\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
-DP NIMs is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP if the composition of
\ell
instances of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. We showed that any NIM that is
(\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
can be modeled by a CM that is
(\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
-DP w.r.t.
f_{\sim}
, which is a first-pair consistent verification function (see the example below Definition
5.12
). Thus, setting
\epsilon_{1}=\dots=\epsilon_{k}=\epsilon_{0}
,
\delta_{1}=\dots=\delta_{k}=\delta_{0}
in Theorem
5.14
recovers and generalizes the classic parallel composition theorem. Our theorem extends differential privacy guarantees to settings where an adversary can adaptively create an unbounded number of NIMs.

6.
Designing Complex Continual Mechanisms

So far we discussed the setting where the messages are issued by an adversary. However,
in the design of differentially private mechanisms in the streaming setting, it is common to use existing differentially private interactive mechanisms, such as the Sparse Vector Technique (SVT) and continual counters, as sub-mechanisms.
Thus, it is the main mechanism
\mathcal{M}
that issues messages to its sub-mechanisms.
The main mechanism may fix the set of sub-mechanisms at initialization or select these mechanisms adaptively over time.
For example, in Section
7
, we present a mechanism that maintains
d
fixed binary counters while adaptively instantiating an unbounded number of SVT and Laplace mechanisms as required.

The privacy analysis of such mechanisms
\mathcal{M}
is challenging as the creation of sub-mechanisms and their inputs is adaptive and dependent. The goal of this section is to give a set of conditions for
\mathcal{M}
and prove a theorem that shows that under these conditions the privacy of
\mathcal{M}
can directly be bounded by applying our concurrent composition theorems for continual mechanisms to the sub-mechanisms. Specifically, given that the
f^{\prime}
-concurrent composition of CMs is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. some verification function
f^{\prime}
, we would like to show that
\mathcal{M}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the given verification function
f
.

As discussed in Section
3
, these sub-mechanisms can be modeled as CMs. We formalize
\mathcal{M}
, which runs multiple CMs, as a continual mechanism of the form
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, where
\mathcal{P}
is an IPM and
ExtConComp
is the CM defined in Section
4.1
. Since
\mathcal{M}
is a CM, its initial state space is a singleton, implying that the initial state space of
\mathcal{P}
is also a singleton. Upon receiving a message,
\mathcal{P}
may create new differentially private CMs or ask queries from the existing ones by communicating with
ExtConComp
, possibly multiple times.

To prove that
\mathcal{M}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
, we need to show that the views of every adversary
\mathcal{A}
interacting with
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable.
Intuitively, the messages that
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
receives from
\mathcal{I}(b)
contain information about
b
. Therefore, to deduce that
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
from the fact that
ExtConComp
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f^{\prime}
, the IPM
\mathcal{P}
must not directly use the messages from
\mathcal{I}
to respond. Instead, it should only use these messages to determine which queries it wants to send to its sub-mechanisms via
ExtConComp
. More precisely,
\mathcal{P}
must generate an answer for
\mathcal{I}
only based on the privatized answers provided by
ExtConComp
and
independent
of the un-privatized input sequence (Response Property). Moreover,
\mathcal{P}
must decide whether to continue interacting with
ExtConComp
or to return a response based on these privatized answers (Destination Property).

Finally, we need a property for
\mathcal{P}
to convert
f
-valid message sequences to
f^{\prime}
-valid ones: Let
m_{1}^{0},m_{2}^{0},\dots
and
m_{1}^{1},m_{2}^{1},\dots
be two sequences such that the sequence
(m_{1}^{0},m_{1}^{1}),(m_{2}^{0},m_{2}^{1}),\dots
is
f
-valid. Upon receiving each sequence
m_{1}^{b},m_{2}^{b},\dots
, the mechanism
\mathcal{P}
must send the messages
m_{1}^{b\prime},m_{2}^{b\prime},\dots
to
ExtConComp
such that
(m_{1}^{0\prime},m_{1}^{1\prime}),(m_{2}^{0\prime},m_{2}^{1\prime}),\dots
is
f^{\prime}
-valid (
f\to f^{\prime}
Property).

In Section
6.1
, we formalize the above properties for
\mathcal{P}
, and in Section
6.2
, we prove the desired privacy guarantees for
\mathcal{M}
. Combining this result with the concurrent composition theorems provided in the previous sections, we can avoid complicated privacy analysis for many CMs. To obtain rigorous results, our statements and proofs must be presented in a technical and somewhat complex form. In contrast, mechanisms are usually described more simply using pseudocode rather than message-passing state machines. In Section
6.3
, we reformulate our results in terms of pseudocode, enabling new mechanisms to be analyzed directly without the need for reformulating to the setting in this paper.

6.1.
Properties.

To formally state that
\mathcal{P}
maps
f
-valid sequences to
f^{\prime}
-valid ones,
we assume that
\mathcal{P}
is deterministic
. This means
\mathcal{P}
can be expressed as a deterministic function that maps the sequence of received messages to its next output message. This assumption is not restrictive, as randomness can be modeled by a
0
-DP sub-mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{i}
that accepts a single message
\top
, and upon receiving it, generates a sufficiently long sequence of random bits or samples from a fixed distribution.

Recall that the goal of this section is to show
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
if
ExtConComp
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f^{\prime}
. That is, we need to show that the views of every adversary interacting with
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable.

The messages received by
\mathcal{P}
from
\mathcal{I}
contain information about the bit used by
\mathcal{I}
to filter the adversary’s pairs. Therefore, to directly use the existing composition theorems for
ExtConComp
to analyze the privacy of
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, we need to ensure that
\mathcal{P}
only utilizes the messages from
\mathcal{I}
to generate queries for the existing private sub-mechanisms executed by
ExtConComp
. Moreover, we need to guarantee that the sequence
(m_{1}^{0\prime},m_{1}^{1\prime}),(m_{2}^{0\prime},m_{2}^{1\prime}),\dots
is
f^{\prime}
-valid, where
m_{1}^{b\prime},m_{2}^{b\prime},\dots
is the sequence of messages that
\mathcal{P}
sends to
ExtConComp
when it receives messages from
\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{V}[f]
. The following properties formalize these:

•

Destination Property.
The destination
L
or
R
of messages sent by
\mathcal{P}
is determined solely based on messages received from its right mechanism (here,
ExtConComp
). This ensures that if two transcripts contain identical sequences of messages from the right mechanism but different sequences from the left mechanism,
\mathcal{P}
will still interact with the same mechanism in the next round.

•

Response Property.
The responses sent by
\mathcal{P}
to the left mechanism depend only on the messages received from the right mechanism. That is, if two transcripts contain identical sequences of messages from the right mechanism and
\mathcal{P}
sends its next message to the left mechanism, then this message is identical in both cases.

•

f\to f^{\prime}
Property.

For
v\in\{{\texttt{L}},{\texttt{R}}\}
, let the pair
(m,v)
denote a message
m
sent to or received from the
v
’th mechanism (i.e., left or right) of
\mathcal{P}
. For
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
\sigma^{b}=\left((m_{1}^{b},v_{1}^{b}),\dots,(m_{t}^{b},v_{t}^{b})\right)
be a sequence of
t
input messages to
\mathcal{P}
, and let
\tau^{b}=\left((m_{1}^{b\prime},v_{1}^{b\prime}),\dots,(m_{t}^{b\prime},v_{t}^{b\prime})\right)
be the corresponding sequence of
t
output messages from
\mathcal{P}
. Suppose the following conditions hold:

(i)

v_{i}^{0}=v_{i}^{1}
for each
i\in[t]
, i.e., the sender of messages is identical;

(ii)

m_{i}^{0}=m_{i}^{1}
for each
i\in[t]
where
v_{i}^{0}=v_{i}^{1}={\texttt{R}}
, meaning that messages from the right mechanism are identical; and

(iii)

the sequence of
(m_{i}^{0},m_{i}^{1})
s for all
i\in[t]
where
v_{i}^{0}=v_{i}^{1}={\texttt{L}}
is
f
-valid.

Then, by the destination property of
\mathcal{P}
,
v_{i}^{0\prime}=v_{i}^{1\prime}
for each
i\in[t]
, and by the response property,
m_{i}^{0\prime}=m_{i}^{1\prime}
for each
i\in[t]
with
v_{i}^{0}=v_{i}^{1}={\texttt{L}}
. The
f\to f^{\prime}
property states that under these conditions, the sequence
\left((m_{i}^{0\prime},m_{i}^{1\prime})\right)
for all
i\in[t]
where
v_{i}^{0\prime}=v_{i}^{1\prime}={\texttt{R}}
must be
f^{\prime}
-valid.

6.2.
Privacy Result.

In this section, we will prove the following theorem:

Theorem 6.1.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a deterministic interactive post-processing mechanism with a singleton initial state space satisfying the destination, response, and
f\to f^{\prime}
properties. If
ExtConComp
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f^{\prime}
against adaptive adversaries, then the continual mechanism
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
against adaptive adversaries.

In Theorem
6.1
, we assume that the views of every adversary interacting with
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable. The goal is to show the same result for
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
. The key idea of the proof is to show that the IMs
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{T}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are identical for some IPM
\mathcal{T}
with a singleton initial state space. The proof consists of two steps.

Step 1 (Swapping
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{I}(b)
).
We start by swapping the positions of
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{I}(b)
in
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
. The verifier
\mathcal{V}[f]
sends pairs of query messages, while
\mathcal{P}
expects a single one. Also, the identifier
\mathcal{I}(b)
expects pairs of messages, while
\mathcal{P}
generates a single response. Therefore, when swapping
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{I}(b)
, we should replace
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)
with
\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}^{2}
, where
\mathcal{P}^{2}
is an interactive post-processing mechanism that runs two instances of
\mathcal{P}
in parallel.
The right mechanism of
\mathcal{P}^{2}
is
\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, and its left mechanism is
\mathcal{V}[f]
. The exact definition of
\mathcal{P}^{2}
is given below.

Definition 6.2 (
\mathcal{P}^{2}
).

Let
\mathcal{P}:S_{\mathcal{P}}\times\left((\{{\texttt{L}}\}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{L})\cup(\{{\texttt{R}}\}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{R})\right)\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{P}}\times\left((\{{\texttt{L}}\}\times A_{\mathcal{P}}^{L})\cup(\{{\texttt{R}}\}\times A_{\mathcal{P}}^{R})\right)
be a deterministic interactive post-processing mechanism with a singleton initial state space
S_{\mathcal{P}}^{\text{init}}=\{s^{\text{init}}\}
, satisfying the destination and response properties.
\mathcal{P}^{2}:S_{\mathcal{P}}^{2}\times\left((\{{\texttt{L}}\}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{L})\cup(\{{\texttt{R}}\}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{R}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{R})\right)\rightarrow S_{\mathcal{P}}\times\left((\{{\texttt{L}}\}\times A_{\mathcal{P}}^{L})\cup(\{{\texttt{R}}\}\times A_{\mathcal{P}}^{R})\right)
is an interactive post-processing mechanism that executes two instances of
\mathcal{P}
, referred to as
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. The state of
\mathcal{P}^{2}
is a pair
(s_{0},s_{1})
denoting the current states of
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. The initial state space of
\mathcal{P}^{2}
is
\{(s^{\text{init}},s^{\text{init}})\}
.

When
\mathcal{P}^{2}
receives a message
m\in Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{L}
from the left mechanism, it forwards two copies of
m
to both
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
as left messages. Specifically, it executes
(s_{0},v_{0}^{\prime},m_{0}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{0}(s_{0},{\texttt{L}},m)
and
(s_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(s_{1},{\texttt{L}},m)
. When
\mathcal{P}^{2}
receives a pair of messages
(m_{0},m_{1})\in Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{R}\times Q_{\mathcal{P}}^{R}
from the right mechanism, it passes each
m_{b}
to
\mathcal{P}_{b}
as a right message. Specifically, it executes
(s_{0},v_{0}^{\prime},m_{0}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{0}(s_{0},{\texttt{R}},m_{0})
and
(s_{1},v_{1}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}(s_{1},{\texttt{R}},m_{0})
. By the destination property of
\mathcal{P}
and the fact that both
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
receive identical left messages, we have
v_{0}^{\prime}=v_{1}^{\prime}
. If
v_{0}^{\prime}=v_{1}^{\prime}={\texttt{R}}
, then
\mathcal{P}^{2}
sends
(m_{0}^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime})
to its right mechanism. If
v_{0}^{\prime}=v_{1}^{\prime}={\texttt{L}}
, then by the response property,
m_{0}^{\prime}=m_{1}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{P}^{2}
returns
m_{0}^{\prime}
to its left mechanism.

The following claim follows directly from the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{2}
:

Claim 6.3.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the interactive mechanisms
\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{P}^{2}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are identical.

Step 2 (Inserting
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]
).
Due to the
f\to f^{\prime}
property, when
\mathcal{P}^{2}
receives an
f
-valid message sequence from its left mechanism, it sends an
f^{\prime}
-valid message sequence to its right mechanism. We insert
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]
between
\mathcal{P}^{2}
and
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{P}^{2}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
. By definition, when
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]
receives an
f^{\prime}
-valid message sequence from its left mechanism, it acts like it does not exist. Specifically, it forwards every message from its left and right mechanism unchanged to the opposite mechanism. Thus, we have:

Claim 6.4.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the interactive mechanisms
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}^{2}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}^{2}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are identical.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}^{2}
in Claim
6.4
is an IPM with a singleton initial state space. Thus, by Lemma
2.16
, the fact that for every adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the views of
\mathcal{A}
interacting with
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable implies that for every adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the views of
\mathcal{A}
interacting with
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable, which finishes the proof.
∎

6.3.
Simplified Formulation for Pseudocodes

Continual mechanisms are typically not described as state machines exchanging messages. Instead, they are presented in the form of pseudocode: at each time step, the pseudocode receives an input, updates its internal variables, and produces an output. For clarity and practicality, we restate the results of this section in the pseudocode framework.

Tainted vs. Untainted Variables.

To rigorously track sensitive data, we rely on concepts from computer security.
Information flow
characterizes how data transfers between variables during a program’s execution
(
5
,
30
)
.
Taint analysis
builds upon this to monitor the propagation of sensitive inputs, known as
taint sources

(
27
,
31
)
. Any variable computed using a taint source becomes a
tainted variable
. To safely release data, it must be processed by a
sanitizer
—in our context, a differentially private submechanism. The sanitizer mathematically interrupts the sensitive information flow, rendering its outputs and all subsequent downstream computations as
untainted variables
.

To formalize this, we partition the variables of a mechanism into two categories using a recursive definition based on their inputs:

•

Tainted variables:
The mechanism’s inputs are strictly tainted. Furthermore, any intermediate variable computed via a function where at least one input argument is a tainted variable is also considered tainted. These variables retain a direct dependence on the raw inputs.

•

Untainted variables:
Variables computed solely from privatized data or fixed values (such as the privacy parameters or constants) are untainted. Crucially, the output of any differentially private submechanism (the sanitizer) is defined as untainted, regardless of whether its inputs were tainted. Any subsequent variable computed as a function of
only
untainted variables remains untainted. Consequently, untainted variables are purely a post-processing of privatized outputs.

Note.

For simplicity, we assume the pseudocode of
\mathcal{M}
is deterministic as any randomness could be modeled as a submechanism call. For instance, sampling from the uniform distribution could be represented as invoking a
0
-DP submechanism that outputs such a sample on a fixed input.

Definition 6.5.

For
t\in\mathbb{N}
, given two sequences
\sigma^{0}=(m_{1}^{0},\dots,m_{t}^{0})
and
\sigma^{1}=(m_{1}^{1},\dots,m_{t}^{1})
, their
combination
is defined as
\big((m_{1}^{0},m_{1}^{1}),\dots,(m_{t}^{0},m_{t}^{1})\big)
. Recall the three properties from Section
6.1
. We reformulate them for pseudocodes as follows:

(1)

Destination property.

A pseudocode satisfies this property if (i) the decision of whether to produce an output, create a new submechanism, or interact with an existing one, (ii) the choice of the new submechanism in the second case, and (iii) the selection of the submechanism to interact with in the third case is made solely based on untainted variables.

(2)

Response property.

A pseudocode satisfies this property if its output is determined solely by untainted variables.

(3)

f\to f^{\prime}
property.

Let
f
and
f^{\prime}
be verification functions. A pseudocode satisfies this property if, for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every pair of input sequences
(\sigma^{0},\sigma^{1})
of length
t
forming an
f
-valid combination, the following holds: Assume that all untainted variables of
\mathcal{M}
are updated identically through the end of time step
t
. For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, define
\sigma^{b}
to denote the sequence of creation queries and inputs to submechanisms when processing
\sigma^{b}
. Here, a creation query is a tuple specifying a created submechanism, its privacy parameters, and associated verification functions. Also, an input
m
to the
j
-th submechanism is represented by the pair
(m,j)
. Then, the combination of
\sigma^{0}
and
\sigma^{1}
is required to be
f^{\prime}
-valid.

Corollary 6.6.

Let
f
and
f^{\prime}
be two verification functions. For
\epsilon\geq 0
and
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, suppose the
f^{\prime}
-concurrent composition of continual mechanisms is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. Then, every continual mechanism whose pseudocode satisfies the destination, response, and
f\to f^{\prime}
properties, defined above, is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
.

7.
Application (Continual Monotone Histogram Query Problem)

We next show an application of our technique to the continual monotone histogram query problem. So far, we defined differential privacy (DP) with respect to (w.r.t.) verification functions. To be consistent with the literature, in this section we use the notion of DP w.r.t. “neighboring input sequences” instead:

Remark 7.1.

Let
\sim
be a binary relation on a family of message sequences. Throughout this section, we say that a mechanism is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim
(or simply it is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP) if the mechanism is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f_{\sim}

against adaptive adversaries
, where
f_{\sim}
is defined as follows: For every message sequence
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
,
f_{\sim}(m_{1}\dots,m_{t})=\top
if and only if the message sequence is suitable in the sense of Definition
4.5
(implying
m_{j}=(m_{j}^{0},m_{j}^{1})
for every
j\in\{1,\dots,t\}
) and the sequences
\sigma_{0}=(m_{1}^{0},\dots,m_{t}^{0})
and
\sigma_{1}=(m_{1}^{1},\dots,m_{t}^{1})
satisfy
\sigma_{0}\sim\sigma_{1}
.

Definition 7.2.

Let
d,T\in\mathbb{N}
and let
q:\mathbb{N}^{d}\to\mathbb{N}
be a function that is monotone in its input. In the (differentially private)
continual monotone histogram query problem
, a continual mechanism
\mathcal{M}
receives
T
inputs of row vectors
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{T})
, each in
\{0,1\}^{d}
. Differential privacy for
\mathcal{M}
is defined with respect to the neighbor relation
\sim_{H}
defined as follows (see Remark
7.1
). For
T\in\mathbb{N}
, two input streams in
\left(\{0,1\}^{d}\right)^{T}
are neighboring if they are identical except for a single step. The output of
\mathcal{M}
at each time step
t
is (an additive approximation to)
q(t)=q((\sum_{\ell=1}^{t}x_{i}^{\ell})_{i\in[d]})
.

Definition 7.3.

Recall function
q:\{0,1\}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and neighbor relation
\sim_{H}
from Definition
7.2
.
q
is said to be
1
-sensitive
if
\max_{x^{0}\sim_{H}x^{1}}|q(x^{0})-q(x^{1})|
is at most
1
.

Henzinger, Sricharan, and Steiner
(
18
)
presented
the best known differentially private mechanism for the monotone histogram query problem, where the query is
1
-sensitive. For
\epsilon
-differential privacy it has, with probability at least
1-\beta
, an additive error of
O((d\log^{2}(dq^{*}/\beta)+\log T)/\epsilon)
, and for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy, it has an additive error of
O((d\log^{1.5}(dq^{*}/\beta)+\log T)\log\frac{1}{\delta}/\epsilon)
, where
q^{*}=\max_{t\in[T]}q(t)
. The privacy of the mechanism is proven “from scratch” using the concept of a privacy game
(
19
)
and the bound was only shown for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP against an oblivious adversary.

In Section
7.1
, we give an overview of the mechanism in
(
18
)
, which we will call
HSS
, and in Section
7.2
, we give a simple privacy proof for it using our approach. With the new privacy analysis, the result for
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differential privacy is stronger and holds against an adaptive adversary.

7.1.
Overview of Mechanism.

The mechanism
HSS
, described in Algorithm
4
, employs a variant of the Sparse Vector Technique mechanism,
SVT
, and a
d
-dimensional counter mechanism,
d-counter
, as submechanisms. We start by defining these mechanisms and then explain
HSS
.

Sparse vector technique mechanism (SVT).

The mechanism
SVT
is described in Algorithm
3
using two operations. The first,
\texttt{SVT-init}(\epsilon,q,h)
, initializes the mechanism with a privacy parameter
\epsilon
, a query
q
, and an initial
d
-dimensional vector
h
. This procedure indeed describes the creation of a continual mechanism
\textrm{SVT}_{h,\epsilon,q}
. The second operation,
\texttt{SVT-update}(x,\texttt{Thresh})
, maps inputs
(x,\texttt{Thresh})\in\{0,1\}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
to outputs in
\{\top,\bot\}
. The mechanism maintains a running sum of all inputs
x
and, in a differentially private manner, checks whether applying
q
to the running sum yields a value at least
Thresh
. If so, it outputs
\top
and halts. Otherwise, it outputs
\bot
and continues. These two procedures are given in detail in Algorithm
3
. The command
\mathrm{Lap}(b)
denotes a random sample from the Laplace distribution with scale parameter
b
.

To clarify the connection between these pseudocodes and our definition of continual mechanisms (Definition
3.1
), we note that the state of
\textrm{SVT}_{h,\epsilon,q}
is the tuple of its variables. When
\texttt{SVT-init}(\epsilon,q,h)
is invoked, the continual mechanism
\textrm{SVT}_{h,\epsilon,q}
with the single initial state
(\epsilon,q,h,\tau=\text{null})
is created. In the pseudocode of
SVT-init
, the variable
\tau
takes a value. In our setting, such initialization is considered to happen when
\textrm{SVT}_{h,\epsilon,q}
receives its first input. The
SVT-update
operation then describes how
\textrm{SVT}_{h,\epsilon,q}
maps its current state (i.e., set of variables) and an input message
m=(x,\texttt{Thresh})\in\{0,1\}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
to a new state (with updated variables) and a response
m^{\prime}\in\{\top,\bot\}
.

Algorithm 3

Sparse vector technique for histogram queries

procedure

SVT-init
(
\epsilon,q,h
):

h_{i}\leftarrow h_{i}
for all
i\in[d]

\epsilon=\epsilon

q=q

\tau\leftarrow\mathrm{Lap}(1/\epsilon)

end

procedure

procedure

SVT-update
(
x,\texttt{Thresh}
):

h_{i}=h_{i}+x_{i}
for all
i\in[d]

if

q(h)+\mathrm{Lap}(2/\epsilon)>\texttt{Thresh}+\tau

then

return
\top
and halt

else

return
\bot

end

if

end

procedure

As part of their privacy proof, the following is shown in
(
18
)
:

Lemma 7.4.

If
q
is a
1
-sensitive function, then for every
\epsilon
and
h
, the mechanism
\textrm{SVT}_{\epsilon/6,q,h}
is
\epsilon/3
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim_{S}
defined as follows (see Remark
7.1
). For
T\in\mathbb{N}
, two input streams
\left((x_{1},\tau_{1}),\dots,(x_{T},\tau_{T})\right)
and
\left((y_{1},\nu_{1}),\dots,(y_{T},\nu_{T})\right)
in
\left((\{0,1\}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})\right)^{T}
are neighboring if the (threshold) sequences
(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{T})
and
(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{T})
are identical at every entry and the (binary) sequences
(x_{1},\dots,x_{T})
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{T})
are identical in all but one entry.

d
-dimensional continual counter.

The mechanism
d-counter
is described by two operations. The first,
\texttt{d-counter-init}(\epsilon)
, creates
d
continual counters that are
\epsilon/d
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim^{\prime}
, where two real-valued sequences of the same length are neighbors if they differ only on a single step by at most
1
. The second,
\texttt{d-counter-update}(c)
, gives the elements of its
d
-dimensional input
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
to their corresponding counters and returns the aggregated outputs as a
d
-dimensional vector. We denote this continual mechanism by
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon}
. In another version,
\texttt{d-counter-init}(\epsilon,\delta)
creates
d
continual counters, each satisfying
(\epsilon/d,\delta/d)
-DP w.r.t.
\sim^{\prime}
. We represent this continual mechanism by
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon,\delta}
.

By definition, mechanisms
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon}
and
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon,\delta}
are the concurrent composition of
d
many
\epsilon/d
- and
(\epsilon/d,\delta/d)
-DP fixed private continual counters. We can assume that these continual counters return an integer value at each point of time, and thus they have discrete answer distributions. If the output space is real, we use a counter with integer outputs that post-processes the original counter by rounding its output up to the nearest integer. By the post-processing lemma, the privacy guarantees still hold and the additive error is only negligibly affected by at most
1
. Therefore, Theorem
4.10
is applicable and by the basic composition, we have:

Corollary 7.5.

Mechanisms
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon}
and
\texttt{d-counter}_{\epsilon,\delta}
are
\epsilon
-DP and
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim_{C}
defined as follows (see Remark
7.1
). For
T\in\mathbb{N}
, two input streams
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{T})
and
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{T})
in
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{T}
are neighboring if there exists
i\in[T]
such that
x_{j}=y_{j}
for all
j\in[T]\setminus\{i\}
and
|x_{i}-y_{i}|_{\infty}\leq 1
, i.e., each entry of
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
differs by at most
1
.

Continual histogram query mechanism.

The mechanism
HSS
, described in Algorithm
4
, is initialized with a privacy parameter
\epsilon>0
, a query
q:\mathbb{N}^{d}\to\mathbb{N}
, and an accuracy parameter
0<\beta\leq 1
and receives a vector
x\in\{0,1\}^{d}
as input at each time step. This mechanism partitions the input stream into subsequences, called
intervals
. At the end of each interval, it forwards the
d
-dimensional sum of all the inputs of the interval to the differentially private
d
-dimensional continual counter
d-counter
. (Note that
d-counter
can be used directly to solve the problem; however,
HSS
achieves better accuracy bounds in many cases.) The mechanism
HSS
maintains two
d
-dimensional vectors: (1) variable
c
, where
c_{i}
stores the exact sum of coordinate
i
within the current interval, and (2) variable
h
, where
h_{i}
stores the approximate sum of coordinate
i
over all
previous
intervals computed by
d-counter
. Both vectors are initialized to
0^{d}
.

At the beginning of each interval (including the initial time step),
HSS
creates a fresh instance of the Sparse Vector Technique mechanism
SVT
with privacy parameter
\epsilon/6
, query
q
, and initial vector
h=0^{d}
(denoted by
\textrm{SVT}_{\epsilon/6,q,h}
). The mechanism discards the previous instance.
HSS
keeps track of the current time step and the current interval using variables
t
and
j
, respectively. In Algorithm
4
,
\gamma
and
\xi
are some deterministic functions, mapping
t,j,\beta,\epsilon
to a real value.

The mechanism
HSS
also stores a threshold variable
Thresh
, initially set to
\gamma(1,1,\beta,\epsilon)
. Upon receiving an input
x
at step
t
, the mechanism updates
c
and invokes
\texttt{SVT-update}(x,\texttt{Thresh})
. If the response, denoted by
a
, is
\top
, the current interval ends and
HSS
takes the following actions: (threshold update) It first privately evaluates
q(c+h)
via the Laplace noise and compares the noisy result to
\texttt{Thresh}-\xi(t,j,\beta,\epsilon)
. If the inequality holds,
Thresh
is suitably increased. Then independent of the outcome of this comparison,
Thresh
is updated again to reflect the increase in the number of intervals. (summation update) Subsequently,
HSS
passes
c
to
d-counter
, updates
h
with the output of
d-counter
, and resets
c
to
0^{d}
. Since
SVT
halts after returning
\top
,
HSS
also creates a new instance
\textrm{SVT}_{\epsilon/6,q,h}
.

Finally, regardless of the output of
\texttt{SVT-update}(x,\texttt{Thresh})
,
HSS
updates
Thresh
once more to account for the increased number of time steps and outputs
h
.

Algorithm 4

Mechanism for answering a monotone histogram query

procedure

Hist-init
(
\epsilon,q,\beta
):

\texttt{d-counter-init}(\epsilon/3)
(or
\texttt{d-counter-init}(\epsilon/3,\delta)
)

h_{i}\leftarrow 0
for all
i\in[d]
,
c_{i}\leftarrow 0
for all
i\in[d]

out\leftarrow q(h)

j\leftarrow 1

\texttt{SVT-init}(\epsilon/6,q,h)

a\leftarrow\bot

\texttt{Thresh}=\gamma(1,j,\beta,\epsilon)

t\leftarrow 0

end

procedure

procedure

Hist-update
(
x
):

t\leftarrow t+1

c_{i}\leftarrow c_{i}+x_{i}
for all
i\in[d]

a=\texttt{SVT-update}(x,\texttt{Thresh})

if

a==\top

then

if

q(c+h)+\mathrm{Lap}(3/\epsilon)>\texttt{Thresh}-\xi(t,j,\beta,\epsilon)

then

\texttt{Thresh}=\texttt{Thresh}+\gamma(t,j,\beta,\epsilon)

end

if

h=\texttt{d-counter-update}(c)

out\leftarrow q(h)

\texttt{SVT-init}(\epsilon/6,q,h)

c_{i}\leftarrow 0
for all
i\in[d]

j\leftarrow j+1

\texttt{Thresh}=\texttt{Thresh}-\gamma(t,j-1,\beta,\epsilon)+\gamma(t,j,\beta,\epsilon)

end

if

\texttt{Thresh}=\texttt{Thresh}-\gamma(t,j,\beta,\epsilon)+\gamma(t+1,j,\beta,\epsilon)

return
out

end

procedure

7.2.
New Privacy Analysis.

In Algorithm
4
, the summation
q(c+h)+\mathrm{Lap}(3/\epsilon)
can be rewritten as an instance of Laplace mechanism with privacy parameter
\epsilon/3
and input
q(c+h)
. The privacy of the Laplace mechanism is defined with respect to the neighbor relation
\sim_{L}
, where two real values
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
r_{1}\sim_{L}r_{2}
if and only if
|r_{1}-r_{2}|\leq 1
. Consequently, the mechanism
HSS
executes:

(1)

one instance of the
d
-dimensional counter
d-counter
, which consists of
d
instances of a scalar counter,

(2)

an arbitrary number of
SVT
instances, and

(3)

an arbitrary number of Laplace mechanisms.

By Corollary
7.5
,
d-counter
is
\epsilon/3
-DP (or
(\epsilon/3,\delta)
-DP when approximate counters are used) w.r.t.
\sim_{C}
. By Lemma
7.4
, each
SVT
instance is
\epsilon/3
-DP w.r.t.
\sim_{S}
. Finally, the Laplace mechanism with parameter
\epsilon/3
is a non-interactive mechanism (NIM) that is well known to satisfy
\epsilon/3
-DP on neighboring real inputs differing by at most
1
(i.e., w.r.t. to neighbor relation
\sim_{L}
defined above). The idea is to show that the concurrent composition of multiple instances of these mechanisms created by
HSS
is
\epsilon
-DP (or
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP when
d-counter
is approx DP). Then, we will show that
HSS
satisfies the required properties in Corollary
6.6
, and thus it has the same privacy guarantees as the concurrent composition. As concurrent composition theorems in this paper require the composed mechanisms to have discrete answer distributions, we first show that we can assume the output space of
d-counter
and Laplace mechanism is discrete. Since
SVT
has a finite output space, its answer distributions are also discrete.

Discretization of outputs.

As argued earlier, the counters executed by
d-counter
output integer values, and thus
d-counter
has discrete answer distributions. Likewise, we can assume that the Laplace mechanism in
HSS
rounds its noisy real-valued output to the nearest integer. By the post-processing lemma, this rounding does not degrade privacy, and by definition, it increases the additive error by at most
1
, which is unimportant for the accuracy guarantees of
(
18
)
. Hence, both
d-counter
and the Laplace mechanism can be treated as having discrete output distributions.

Pseudocode privacy analysis.

Recall from Section
6.3
that untainted variables of a mechanism are (i) outputs of submechanisms, (ii) fixed values (e.g., privacy and accuracy parameters), or (iii) variables computed solely as functions of other untainted variables. In Algorithm
4
, the variables
\epsilon
,
\beta
,
q
,
a
,
h
,
out
,
j
,
t
, and
Thresh
are untainted, while
c
is not: The values of the variables
\epsilon
,
\beta
, and
q
are fixed and independent of the sensitive data. Variables
a
and
h
denote the privatized outcomes of
SVT
and
d-counter
, respectively. Variable
out
equals
q(h)
, where
h
is an untainted variable. Index
j
is initially
1
and is increased by
1
when
a=\top
. Time step
t
is initially
0
and is increased by
1
at each step, independent of the input and other variables. Finally, variable
Thresh
is initialized and updated to values determined by the untainted variables
\epsilon,\beta,j,t
. It is important to note that the decision of updating variables
h
(and consequently
out
) and
Thresh
depends only on the privatized output of
SVT
, i.e., the untainted variable
a
.

We first show that the destination property holds. For that we have to show that the decision of whether to produce an output, create a new submechanism (and if so, which one) or interact with an existing one (and if so, which one) is solely based on untainted variables. All submechanisms of
HSS
are instantiated either in the initial step (and, thus, whether it receives input is independent of tainted variables and the inputs
x
) or based on outputs of the private submechanism
SVT
. The active
SVT
instance receives input at every step, and thus whether it receives input is independent of tainted variables and the inputs
x
. Also, whether the mechanism
d-counter
gets updated depends only on the privatized output of
SVT
. Finally,
HSS
returns an output after the final update of
Thresh
, without any condition on the inputs
x
or the tainted variables. Thus, the
destination property
in Corollary
6.6
holds. Moreover, the final output of
HSS
is
out
, which is an untainted variable, so the
response property
in that corollary also holds. It remains to show
HSS
satisfies the
f\to f^{\prime}
property, where
f
is the verification function corresponding to the neighbor relation
\sim_{H}
in Definition
7.2
and
f^{\prime}
is a verification function satisfying that the
f^{\prime}
-concurrent composition of the continual mechanisms is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Let
T\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{T})
and
y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{T})
be two sequences in
\left(\{0,1\}^{d}\right)^{T}
satisfying
x\sim_{H}y
, i.e., there exists
i\in[T]
such that
x_{j}=y_{j}
for all
j\in[T]\setminus\{i\}
. Fix arbitrary values for all untainted variables at every time step. Conditioned on these values, we compare the input streams of
HSS
’s submechanisms when this mechanisms runs on
x
and
y
:

•

Inputs of
d-counter
:

By construction, the input stream of
HSS
is partitioned into intervals determined by the time steps at which
SVT
outputs
\top
. Since these outputs are untainted, they are identical in both executions to the fixed values specified in the condition. Let
1\leq t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}\leq T
be the steps
SVT
outputs
\top
, and set
t_{0}=0
. The submechanism
d-counter
receives the interval sums
\left(\sum_{\ell=t_{j-1}+1}^{t_{j}}x_{\ell}\right)_{j=1}^{k}
and
\left(\sum_{\ell=t_{j-1}+1}^{t_{j}}y_{\ell}\right)_{j=1}^{k}
as input sequence when
HSS
runs on
x
and
y
, respectively. All intervals not containing
i
have identical sums. If
i>t_{k}
, no (closed) interval includes
i
and thus all sums coincide. If
i\leq t_{k}
, let
j^{*}\in[k]
denote the index satisfying
t_{j^{*}-1}+1\leq i\leq t_{j^{*}}
. Then

\sum_{\ell=t_{j^{*}-1}+1}^{t_{j^{*}}}x_{\ell}-\sum_{\ell=t_{j^{*}-1}+1}^{t_{j^{*}}}y_{\ell}=x_{i}-y_{i}.

Since
x_{i},y_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, we have
x_{i}-y_{i}\in\{-1,0,1\}^{d}
and thus
\|x_{i}-y_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Hence the inputs to
d-counter
differ in at most one coordinate by at most one, i.e., they are
\sim_{C}
-neighbors (see Corollary
7.5
).

•

Inputs of
SVT
:
The threshold variable
Thresh
is untainted. Let
\texttt{Thresh}_{\ell}
denote its fixed value at time
\ell
. Let time steps
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
be defined as above. Each time
SVT
outputs
\top
, the mechanism
HSS
reinitializes it. Therefore,
HSS
initiates
k+1
instances of
SVT
. Let
t_{0}=0
and
t_{k+1}=T
. For each
j\in[k+1]
, the
j
-th instance of
SVT
receives
\left((x_{\ell},\texttt{Thresh}_{\ell})\right)_{\ell=t_{j-1}+1}^{t_{j}}
and
\left((y_{\ell},\texttt{Thresh}_{\ell})\right)_{\ell=t_{j-1}+1}^{t_{j}}
as input sequence when
HSS
runs on
x
and
y
, respectively.
All instances except possibly
j^{*}
(the one containing
i
) receive identical inputs. Let
j^{*}\in[k+1]
denote the index where
t_{j^{*}-1}+1\leq i\leq t_{j^{*}}
. For every
j\in[k+1]\setminus\{j^{*}\}
, the input sequences of the
j
-th
SVT
submechanism are identical when
HSS
runs on
x
and
y
. Moreover, the input sequences of the
j^{*}
-th
SVT
submechanism are identical except for the inputs
(x_{i},\texttt{Thresh}_{i})
and
(y_{i},\texttt{Thresh}_{i})
, which have the same threshold
\texttt{Thresh}_{i}
. Hence, these input sequences are
\sim_{S}
-neighbors (see Lemma
7.4
).

•

Inputs of the Laplace Mechanism:
The Laplace mechanism is applied to the value
q(h+c)
, where
c
is the interval sum. The variable
h
is untainted and therefore fixed and identical in both executions of
HSS
. As shown above, all interval sums coincide except possibly one, and that one differs by at most
1
in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm. Hence the corresponding values
h+c
also differ by at most
1
in
\ell_{\infty}
-norm. Since
q
is
1
-sensitive (see Definition
7.3
), the real-valued outputs of
q
on these inputs differ by at most
1
. Therefore, when
HSS
runs on
x
and
y
, all corresponding Laplace submechanisms receive identical inputs, except possibly one pair whose inputs differ by at most
1
, that is, they are
\sim_{L}
-neighbors.

Hence, the concurrent composition of all submechanisms of
HSS
can be decomposed into three concurrent compositions: (1) all Laplace mechanisms, (2) all
SVT
instances, and (3) the
d
continual counters forming
d-counter
.
The first two are the parallel compositions of purely differentially private mechanisms. By Corollary
5.5
, both are
\epsilon/3
-DP. The third one is, by construction, the concurrent composition of
d
fixed continual counters. In Corollary
7.5
, we showed that this composition, i.e.,
d-counter
, is
\epsilon/3
-DP (or
(\epsilon/3,\delta)
-DP when approx DP counters are used).

By basic composition and Theorem
4.10
, the overall concurrent composition of all three groups of mechanisms is
\epsilon
-DP when
d-counter
uses pure counters, and
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP when approximate counters are used. (More precisely, it is DP w.r.t. a verification function
f^{\prime}
that ensures all submechanisms but one
SVT
instance, one Laplace mechanism, and the
d
counters of
d-counter
are assigned pairs of identical inputs, while the first and second inputs for each of these mechanisms form neighboring sequences.)

By Corollary
6.6
, since
HSS
satisfies the destination, response, and neighboring-mapping properties, and since the concurrent composition of its submechanisms is
\epsilon
-DP (or
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP), the mechanism
HSS
satisfies the same privacy guarantee. We recall that all privacy guarantees in this section hold against adaptive adversaries.

8.
Analyzing Local Differential Privacy

So far, we have discussed the application of concurrent composition theorems in designing continual mechanisms with modular structures. Another natural application of these theorems arises in the setting of
local differential privacy
(LDP), where multiple users interact with an untrusted server over (possibly) multiple rounds. Each user holds a private data record (or dataset) and, upon receiving a query from the server, produces a response that satisfies differential privacy. After several rounds of interaction, the server aggregates the received messages and outputs an estimate of a function over the users’ data. Formally, a
local protocol
consists of an interactive mechanism for the server and an interactive mechanism for each user. Local differential privacy is defined with respect to a neighbor relation on sequences of datasets held by the users.

Definition 8.1.

Let
P
be a local protocol with
n
users. Let
\mathcal{X}
denote the domain of datasets held by the users, and let
\sim
be a neighbor relation on
\mathcal{X}^{n}
. For example, two dataset sequences
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
and
x^{\prime}=(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n}^{\prime})
may satisfy
x\sim x^{\prime}
if they differ in exactly one coordinate
i
, where
x_{i}\sim^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}
for a neighbor relation
\sim^{*}
on
\mathcal{X}
. The protocol
P
is said to satisfy
(\epsilon,\delta)
-
local differential privacy
(or
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP) w.r.t.
\sim
if, for every pair of neighboring dataset sequences
x\sim x^{\prime}
, the distributions of the server’s view—consisting of its random coins and the transcript of all exchanged messages—are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable when users hold the datasets in
x
and
x^{\prime}
.

Equivalence of a local protocol
P
being LDP to a continual mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{P}
being DP.

Let
P
be a local protocol as defined above, and let
\sim
denote the neighboring relation on the sequences of users’ datasets. Define
f_{\sim}
to output
\top
only when it receives a single message of the form
(x,x^{\prime})
where
x\sim x^{\prime}
. We construct a continual mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{P}
that receives a dataset sequence as (a single) input, simulates the interaction of the server with the users in the protocol
P
, and returns the view of the server as output. (Indeed,
\mathcal{M}_{P}
is a NIM, which is a special case of CMs.) We will show that
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim
if and only if
\mathcal{M}_{P}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f_{\sim}
. We define
\mathcal{M}_{P}
as

\mathcal{M}_{P}=\mathcal{S}_{P}\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}},

where
\mathcal{S}_{P}
is an IPM modeling the server’s behavior.
The mechanism
\mathcal{S}_{P}
is specifically defined as follows:
\mathcal{S}_{P}
accepts only one left message of the form
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, where each
x_{i}
is a dataset for user
i
. Upon receiving
x
,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
takes the following actions: (1) It creates
n
user mechanisms by sending appropriate creation queries to
ExtConComp
, and (2) it sends each
x_{i}
as the first message to the corresponding user mechanism through
ExtConComp
. (3) It then interacts with the user mechanisms according to the server’s interaction rules defined in protocol
P
. (4) Once the server decides to halt,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
outputs the server’s view as its (left) response and then terminates. This view consists of the server’s internal randomness (i.e., random coins used by
\mathcal{S}_{P}
) and the transcript of all messages sent and received by the server. Thus, it does not include the creation queries or the initial dataset messages sent by
\mathcal{S}_{P}
.

Claim 8.2.

The local protocol
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t. the neighboring relation
\sim
if and only if the CM
\mathcal{M}_{P}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f_{\sim}
.

Proof.

The claim holds by the construction of
\mathcal{M}_{P}
and Definition
8.1
.
∎

This equivalence allows us to analyze the privacy of the protocol
P
by examining the privacy of the corresponding CM
\mathcal{M}_{P}
, which has a modular design, using Theorem
6.1
.

Remark 8.3.

While the server in
P
may use randomness, Theorem
6.1
requires
\mathcal{S}_{P}
to be deterministic. This can be handled by defining
\mathcal{S}_{P}
to request
ExtConComp
to create a
(0,0)
-DP mechanism that generates random coins or random samples whenever queried.

Claim 8.4.

The IPM
\mathcal{S}_{P}
satisfies the destination and response properties defined in Section
6.1
and required by Theorem
6.1
.

Proof.

By construction, upon receiving the left message
x
, the IPM
\mathcal{S}_{P}
in
\mathcal{M}_{P}
first sends exactly
2n
messages to
ExtConComp
:
n
creation queries and
n
dataset messages. Although the content of these dataset messages depends on
x
, the decision of
\mathcal{S}_{P}
sending a dataset message to its right mechanism,
ExtConComp
, is completely independent of
x
. (If the server is randomized,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
additionally creates an auxiliary
(0,0)
-DP mechanism. This creation query to
ExtConComp
is also independent of
x
.)

After these initial interactions,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
proceeds to interact with the user mechanisms according to the server’s specification in
P
. The decision of whether to continue the interaction or to return a left response and halt is solely determined by the control flow of the server, which depends only on the users’ (and the auxiliary mechanism’s) responses and not directly on
x
. (The server has no access to
x
.) Hence,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
satisfies the destination property.

Moreover, the (single) left response of
\mathcal{S}_{P}
is the server’s view, consisting of the messages exchanged with (the auxiliary mechanism and) the user mechanisms after their dataset is set. By design, this depends only on the privatized outputs of the mechanisms executed by
ExtConComp
. Therefore,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
also satisfies the response property.
∎

To apply Theorem
6.1
, it remains to show that for a verification function
f
, the IPM
\mathcal{S}_{P}
satisfies the
f_{\sim}\to f
property (see Section
6.1
) and the
f
-concurrent composition of CMs is DP. We now propose a verification function
f
for which the
f_{\sim}\to f
property holds. Intuitively, this verification function enforces that all user mechanisms are first created, and that each is assigned a pair of datasets
(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})
satisfying
x\sim x^{\prime}
. After this, queries
q
can be asked concurrently from the users. Local protocols are typically defined so that the queries asked from each mechanism satisfy certain properties
\mathrm{prop}
, which play a key role in the privacy analysis. To later be able to show privacy guarantees for the
f
-concurrent composition of CMs, we also define
f
to enforce the queries asked from each user to satisfy
\mathrm{prop}
. The formal definition of
f
is given below. For convenience, we refer to the
i
-th created mechanism as user
i
.

Definition 8.5 (
\sim_{i}
,
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
, and
f_{P,\sim}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\delta_{n}}
).

Let
P
be a local protocol with
n
users, where the queries issued by the server to each user
i\in[n]
satisfy a property
\mathrm{prop}_{i}
. Let
\sim
denote a neighboring relation on the sequences of users’ datasets. For every
i\in[n]
, define the neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
on datasets of user
i
as follows: for datasets
x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime}
, we have
x_{i}\sim_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}
if and only if there exist datasets
x_{j},x_{j}^{\prime}
for all
j\in[n]\setminus\{i\}
such that
x\sim x^{\prime}
. (For example, assume
x
represents a graph with
n
vertices, where
x_{i}
denotes the set of vertices adjacent to vertex
i
. Suppose
x\sim x^{\prime}
iff the corresponding graphs of
x
and
x^{\prime}
differ by an edge. Then
x_{i}\sim_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}
iff the adjacency sets
x_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}
of vertex
i
differ in the presence or absence of a single vertex.)

Define the verification function
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
to accept a message sequence if and only if (1) the first message is a dataset pair
(x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})
with
x_{i}\sim_{i}x_{i}^{\prime}
, and (2) all subsequent messages are pairs of identical queries
(q,q)
, where the sequence of
q
values satisfies
\mathrm{prop}_{i}
.

For every
i\in[n]
, let
\mathcal{M}_{i}
denote the CM associated with user
i
and let
s_{i}
be its single initial state. Assume
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t.
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
.
Then the verification function
f_{P,\sim}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\delta_{n}}
is defined as follows: for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and message sequence
(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})
, we have
f_{P,\sim}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\delta_{n}}(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})=\top
if and only if

(i)

the sequence
(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})
is suitable; and

(ii)

it is a prefix of a sequence consisting of the following messages, in order:

(a)

pairs of identical creation queries
(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i})
for each user
i\in[n]
, where
\alpha_{i}=(\mathcal{M}_{i},s_{i},\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i},f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}})
(and possibly an additional pair for an auxiliary sample-generating mechanism if the server is randomized);

(b)

dataset-assignment messages
((x_{i},i),(x_{i}^{\prime},i))
for each user
i
, where
x\sim x^{\prime}
; and

(c)

arbitrarily many identical query messages
((q,i),(q,i))
directed to the same user
i
.

Note that condition (i) ensures that the query sequence for each user
i
remains valid with respect to
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
.

Claim 8.6.

The IPM
\mathcal{S}_{P}
satisfies the
f_{\sim}\to f_{P,\sim}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\delta_{n}}
property.

Proof.

By definition, every
f_{\sim}
-valid message sequence consists of a single message of the form
(x,x^{\prime})
with
x\sim x^{\prime}
. Consider two executions of
\mathcal{S}_{P}
receiving
x
and
x^{\prime}
as their respective left messages. By definition, both IPMs first identically create user mechanisms. Then one instance assigns datasets
x_{i}
to the users by sending the right messages
(x_{i},i)
, while the other assigns datasets
x_{i}^{\prime}
by sending
(x_{i}^{\prime},i)
. Once all datasets have been assigned, the future behavior of both instances becomes independent of their left inputs—their actions are determined solely by the server description in
P
, which has no access to the datasets and depends only on the (privatized) responses returned by the user mechanisms. Hence, if both instances of
\mathcal{S}_{P}
receive identical right messages (from user mechanisms), they generate identical right queries to the same users, i.e., identical messages of the form
(q,i)
. Consequently, the sequence
\sigma
formed by pairing the right messages produced by these two instances satisfies condition (ii) in Definition
8.5
. We will show that this sequence also satisfies condition (i) in this definition, and thus it is
f_{P,\sim}
-valid. Consequently,
\mathcal{S}_{P}
satisfies the
f_{\sim}\to f_{P,\sim}
property.

For condition (i), we recall that a message sequence is
suitable
if each message is either (i) a pair of identical creation queries
(\mathcal{M},s,\epsilon,\delta,f)^{2}
, where the CM
\mathcal{M}
with single initial state
s
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f
, or (ii) a message of the form
\big((q_{0},\ell),(q_{1},\ell)\big)
, where at least
\ell
mechanisms have already been created and
q_{0},q_{1}
are valid inputs for the
\ell
-th one. Let
\mathcal{M}_{\ell}
denote the
\ell
-th created mechanism and let
f_{\ell}
be its associated verification function. The suitability condition also requires that the sequence of query pairs
(q_{0},q_{1})
extracted from messages of the form
\big((q_{0},\ell),(q_{1},\ell)\big)
be
f_{\ell}
-valid. By construction, the paired sequence
\sigma
satisfies these conditions and is therefore suitable, finishing the proof.
∎

Therefore, by Claim
8.4
and Claim
8.6
, Theorem
6.1
is applicable. This theorem combined with Claim
8.2
implies:

Theorem 8.7.

Let
P
be a local protocol with
n
users, where the queries asked by the server of each user
i\in[n]
satisfy a property
\mathrm{prop}_{i}
. Let
\sim
be a neighbor relation on sequences of
n
user datasets, and for each
i\in[n]
, define the verification function
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
as in Definition
8.5
. Suppose that for parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\epsilon\geq 0
and
0\leq\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n},\delta\leq 1
, the following hold:

(i)

for every
i\in[n]
, the CM corresponding to user
i
is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t.
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
; and

(ii)

the
f_{P,\sim}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{n},\delta_{n}}
-concurrent composition of CMs is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Then the protocol
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim
.

We next simplify this result for a choice of the neighboring relation
\sim
based on a shared neighbor relation for the users. Let
\sim^{*}
denote a neighbor relation on (individual) users’ datasets. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\sim
on sequences of
n
user datasets as follows: For
x,x^{\prime}
, we have
x\sim x^{\prime}
if and only if they differ in at most
k
indices, and for each differing index
i
, we have
x_{i}\sim^{*}x_{i}^{\prime}
. We refer to
\sim
as the
k
-sparse parallel-composition neighbor relation with user-neighbor relation
\sim^{*}
. We note that the relation
\sim^{*}
is implicitly assumed to be reflexive, meaning each dataset is related to itself. Hence, the neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
associated to each user
i
in Definition
8.5
equals
\sim^{*}
.

Corollary 8.8.

Let
P
be a local protocol with
n
users following identical procedures, and let
\sim^{*}
be a neighbor relation on individual users’ datasets. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\sim_{k,\sim^{*}}
denote the
k
-sparse parallel-composition relation with user-neighbor relation
\sim^{*}
, defined above. Assume that in protocol
P
, the queries asked by the server of each user satisfy a property
\mathrm{prop}^{*}
. Let
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
,
\epsilon\geq 0
, and
0\leq\delta^{\prime},\delta\leq 1
. Suppose the following conditions hold:

•

The CM corresponding to each user is
(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f_{\sim^{*},\mathrm{prop}^{*}}
from Definition
8.5
.

•

The composition of
k
instances of the randomized response mechanism
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Then the protocol
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim
.

To prove Corollary
8.8
, we need the following observation:

Observation 8.9.

Let
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
be verification functions such that every message sequence that is
f_{1}
-valid is also
f_{2}
-valid. If a CM
\mathcal{M}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f_{2}
, then
\mathcal{M}
is also
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t.
f_{1}
.

Proof.

By assumption, the IPMs
\mathcal{V}[f_{1}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{V}[f_{1}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{V}[f_{2}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}
are equivalent. Hence, by the post-processing lemma, the claim follows immediately.
∎

Proof of Corollary
8.8
.

Recall the verification function
f^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
from Definition
5.13
. This verification function permits the creation of an arbitrary number of CMs over time, but guarantees that at most
k
of them—each with privacy parameters
(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
—are assigned pairs of non-identical inputs. Moreover, it enforces that if the first message directed to a mechanism contains identical inputs, then all subsequent messages to that mechanism must also contain identical inputs. Comparing the verification functions
f_{P,\sim}^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}}
(Definition
8.5
) and
f^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
, we observe that every
f_{P,\sim}^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}}
-valid message sequence is also
f^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
-valid. Applying Observation
8.9
with
\mathcal{M}={\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, we conclude that if the
f^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
-concurrent composition of CMs is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then so is the
f_{P,\sim}^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}}
-concurrent composition of CMs.

By Theorem
5.14
, if the composition of
k
randomized response mechanisms
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then the
f^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
-concurrent composition of CMs is also
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. Hence, under the second assumption of Corollary
8.8
, the
f_{P,\sim}^{(\epsilon^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}}
-concurrent composition is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. Furthermore, by the definition of
\sim
, for each
i\in[n]
, the neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
in Definition
8.5
coincides with
\sim^{*}
. Thus, the first assumption of the corollary is equivalent to requiring that each user’s CM is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t.
f_{\sim_{i},\mathrm{prop}_{i}}
. Thus, applying Theorem
8.7
yields that the protocol
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 8.10.

In real-world deployments, users may join or leave the system dynamically, and data records may be shared across a subset of users. Furthermore, a user’s local dataset may evolve over time. To cover these scenarios, users are CMs rather than IMs, and in the above discussions, instead of
\mathcal{M}_{P}
receiving only a single message
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, this mechanism receives creation queries for creating a new user, deletion messages informing that a specific user is gone, and messages of the form
(u,j)
requesting
\mathcal{M}_{P}
to forward the dataset-update
u
to user
j
. Without repeating the whole argument, we note that Theorem
8.7
and Corollary
8.8
can be adapted and applied in this general scenario and our concurrent composition theorems for CMs (rather than IMs) can be used to simplify the privacy analysis of these local protocols.

Remark 8.11.

In this section, we assumed that all components (i.e., the server and the users) are honest, meaning that they follow the local protocol. We also assumed that there is no collusion, that is, parties do not share their internal states with each other. Suppose now that
k
users hold private data, while the remaining
n-k
users and the server may be dishonest and collude to learn this data. We can model the colluding parties as a single adversary that interacts concurrently with the
k
target users. In this case, privacy of the local protocol follows from the concurrent composition of the IMs corresponding to these
k
users. If the users’ datasets evolve over time, then the concurrent composition of CMs must be used instead.

9.
Application (Core Decomposition via Multi-Dimensional SVT)

In this section, we review the local protocol of Dhulipala, Henzinger, Li, Liu, Sricharan, and Zhu
(
6
)
and demonstrate how its privacy analysis can be simplified using our concurrent parallel composition theorem for IMs. They study the
core decomposition problem
in the LDP model, where each user corresponds to a (publicly known) vertex of a graph and holds the set of adjacent vertices as its private dataset. A core decomposition protocol is said to satisfy
(\epsilon,\delta)
-
edge-LDP
if it is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim
, where two sequences of user datasets are considered neighbors whenever the corresponding graphs differ by at most one edge.

The core decomposition problem.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph, where
V(G)
and
E(G)
denote its vertex and edge sets, respectively. For a subset
U\subseteq V(G)
, the subgraph of
G
induced by
U
is denoted by
G[U]
. The degree of a vertex
v\in V(G)
is written as
\deg_{G}(v)
. The
core number
of a vertex
v
, denoted
k_{G}(v)
, is the largest integer
k
such that
v
belongs to a subgraph
H\subseteq G
in which every vertex
u\in V(H)
satisfies
\deg_{H}(u)\geq k
. The goal of the core decomposition problem is to compute the
coreness vector

\vec{k}_{G}=(k_{G}(v):v\in V),

which assigns to each vertex its coreness value.

Overview of the Local Protocol by
(
6
)
.

Dhulipala et al.
(
6
)
propose a local protocol
P
for the core decomposition problem, inspired by the classical
peeling algorithm
. Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph stored by
n=|V|
users: each user corresponds to exactly one vertex
v\in V
and stores a private dataset
N_{v}
, defined as

N_{v}=\left\{u\in V\mid\{v,u\}\in E\right\}.

The server knows only the vertex set
V
, while each user
v
knows both
V
and its private set of neighbors
N_{v}
. Next, we will describe the server and user mechanisms in
P
.

Server.

The server maintains four variables: (1) the current core value
d
, initialized to
1
; (2) the active vertex set
A
, initialized to
V
; and (3) the estimated coreness vector
\vec{k}
, initialized to
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The server repeats the following procedure
n
times:
