(1)

Send the current active vertex set
A
and core value
d
to all users
v\in A
and ask whether their number of adjacent vertices in
A
is less than
d
, i.e.,
|N_{v}\cap A|<d
.

(2)

Let
S
denote the subset of vertices in
A
responding with
\top
, indicating that
|N_{v}\cap A|<d
. Update
\vec{k}
by assigning
k_{v}=d
for all
v\in S
.

(3)

Remove the vertices in
S
from
A
.

Users.

For each
v\in V
, let
\mathcal{M}_{v}
denote the user IM corresponding to
v
. This mechanism is a variant of the
Sparse Vector Technique (SVT)
that holds
N_{v}
as its dataset and maps inputs
(A,d)
—where
A\subseteq V
and
d\in\mathbb{N}
—to either
\top
or
\bot
, indicating whether
|N_{v}\cap A|<d
. Two datasets are neighbors if they differ in at most one vertex, i.e., the adjacency set of
v
changes in at most one element. The integer
d
is a threshold, and the set
A
represents a function
f_{A}(\cdot)=|\cdot\cap A|
, which is
1
-sensitive because it changes by at most one on neighboring vertex sets. Modeling
\mathcal{M}_{v}
as a continual mechanism, differential privacy for
\mathcal{M}_{v}
is defined with respect to a verification function
g
that requires the first message to be a pair of identical or neighboring vertex sets, and every subsequent message to be a pair of identical vertex sets (representing
1
-sensitive functions). We say that
\mathcal{M}_{v}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP if it satisfies
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP with respect to
g
against adaptive adversaries.

Lemma 9.1 (
(
6
)
).

Consider the local protocol
P
for the core decomposition problem described above. If every user (SVT) mechanism satisfies
(\epsilon/2,0)
-DP, then the protocol
P
satisfies
(\epsilon,0)
-LDP. Moreover, for a graph
G=(V,E)
with
n=|V|
and maximum degree
\Delta
, the following holds with high probability: for every vertex
v\in V
, the coreness value estimated by the server is in the range
k_{G}(v)\pm O(\log n\log\Delta)
where
k_{G}(v)
is the true core number of
v
.

To prove the privacy part of this lemma, Dhulipala et al.
(
6
)
analyze the privacy of a mechanism called
Multi-Dimensional SVT
, which generalizes SVT by mapping, at each step, a vector of
n

1
-sensitive functions and a vector of
n
thresholds to an element of
\{\top,\bot\}^{n}
. Their analysis is from scratch and technically involved. We observe that multi-dimensional SVT is a special case of the concurrent parallel composition of
n
SVT mechanisms, where the adversary must issue queries to the SVT instances in a round-robin pattern. So we can simplify their analysis using Corollary
5.15
and Theorem
6.1
. However, to illustrate the use of Corollary
8.8
, we instead analyze the entire local protocol directly.

Simplified Privacy Analysis.

Recall that in the local model, a graph with public vertex set
V
of size
n=|V|
is represented as a sequence of adjacency sets
(N_{v})_{v\in V}
, where each
N_{v}
corresponds to the dataset of the user mechanism associated with vertex
v
. Let
\sim^{*}
denote the neighbor relation for user SVT mechanisms. (For every
N,N^{\prime}\subseteq V
, we have
N\sim^{*}N^{\prime}
iff the sets differ in at most one vertex.) Let
\sim_{2,\sim^{*}}
denote the
2
-sparse parallel-composition neighbor relation with user-neighbor relation
\sim^{*}
. By definition, the local protocol
P
of
(
6
)
satisfies
(\epsilon,\delta)
-edge-LDP if, for every two sequences of
n
(adjacency) vertex sets whose graphs differ in exactly one edge, the server’s views are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable. Since such sequences differ in at most two adjacency sets, it suffices to show that
P
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim_{2,\sim^{*}}
.

By construction, functions corresponding to the vertex sets
A
sent by the server are all
1
-sensitive; let this property be
\mathrm{prop}^{*}
. Hence, each user’s SVT mechanism is
(\epsilon/2,0)
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f_{\sim^{*},\mathrm{prop}^{*}}
from Definition
8.5
. By basic composition, the composition of two instances of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon/2,0}
is
(\epsilon,0)
-DP. Therefore, by Corollary
8.8
, the protocol
P
is
(\epsilon,0)
-LDP w.r.t.
\sim_{2,\sim^{*}}
, and therefore
(\epsilon,0)
-edge-LDP.

10.
Reduction to Interactive Randomized Response

Lyu
(
24
)
shows that for every
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP IM
\mathcal{M}
and every pair of neighboring initial states
s_{0},s_{1}
for
\mathcal{M}
, there exists an IPM
\mathcal{P}
such that for each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the mechanisms
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
and
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
are equivalent. This means that
\mathcal{P}
can simulate
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
given access only to the output of a randomized response mechanism
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
. In this section, inspired by this work, we construct a new IPM
\mathcal{P}
that, rather than simulating
\mathcal{M}
based on the outcome of a single instance of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
, operates on the output of
\mathrm{RR}_{0,\delta}
and only when necessary uses the output of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,0}
.
In other words, instead of interacting with the NIM
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
,
\mathcal{P}
interacts with the IM
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
, and under certain conditions, it queries
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
at most once.

In addition, our construction removes or relaxes the following implicit assumptions in Lyu’s proof:

•

Lyu’s result relies on the existence of a constant
T
that upper bounds the number of queries adversaries ask. This restriction comes from a backward definition of a so-called control function. We define a function which is the limit over this control function and, using monotone convergence, show that this “limit function” satisfies all the necessary conditions to be used in the rest of Lyu’s proof. This extends the result to an unbounded number of rounds of interaction.

•

Lyu’s proof uses an algorithmic construction iterating over the answer set, implying that the answer set must be finite. Instead with a mathematical approach, we extend the proof to work with a countably infinite answer set.

Our result is formalized in Lemma
5.6
, which is restated below:

Lemma 0 (Restatement of Lemma
5.6
).

For
\epsilon>0
and
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, let
\mathcal{M}:S_{\mathcal{M}}\times Q_{\mathcal{M}}\to S_{\mathcal{M}}\times A_{\mathcal{M}}
be an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP IM w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
. Suppose that
\mathcal{M}
has discrete answer distributions. Then, for every two neighboring initial states
s_{0}
and
s_{1}
, there exists an IPM
\mathcal{P}
such that for each
b\in\{0,1\}
:

•

The mechanisms
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
and
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
are equivalent.

•

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and every query sequence
(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})\in Q_{\mathcal{M}}^{k}
, if the distributions of answers produced by
\mathcal{M}(s_{0})
and
\mathcal{M}(s_{1})
to
(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})
are identical, then when
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
receives the queries
q_{1},\dots,q_{k}
, the IPM
\mathcal{P}
interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
only once. Otherwise,
\mathcal{P}
interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
at most twice.

Notation 10.1.

For short, let
Q=Q_{\mathcal{M}}
and
A=A_{\mathcal{M}}
. For each
t\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, we define the function
\mu_{t}^{b}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]
such that
\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
denotes the probability of
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
returning answers
a_{1},\dots,a_{t}
to the queries
q_{1},\dots,q_{t}
.

Fixing an upper bound
T
on the number of queries, Lyu
(
24
)
reduces the construction of
\mathcal{P}
to defining a sequence of functions
\phi_{t}^{0}
and
\phi_{t}^{1}
for
t\in[T]
that satisfy three conditions. They then inductively construct
\phi_{t}^{b}
and verify that it satisfies the required conditions. The definition of these conditions is based on a sequence of control function
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{0}
and
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{1}
, which have backward recursive definitions for
t
from
T
to
1
.

In Section
10.1
, we overview the definition of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
and its properties. In Section
10.1.1
, we introduce a new control function
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
with no dependency on
T
. The goal is to use
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
instead of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
. In Section
10.1.2
, we show some properties for
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
, required in later proofs.

In Section
10.2
, we summarize the reduction introduced in
(
24
)
, with the control functions
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
replaced by
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
. We also modify this reduction by adding a new condition to the original three that the functions
\phi_{t}
must satisfy. This additional condition is used to limit the number of interactions between
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
. Finally, in Section
10.3
, we provide a new construction of
\phi_{t}
and show that it satisfies all four conditions.

10.1.
Control Functions
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
and
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
.

Lyu
(
24
)
introduces the following control function:

Definition 10.2 (
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
).

For
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, and
t\in[T]
, the function
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}:(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q\to[0,1]
is defined recursively as follows: for each transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})=\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{a_{t}\in A}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t},q_{t+1}),&t<T\\
\sum\limits_{a_{t}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\},&t=T\end{cases}

We introduce another control function
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
based on
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
:

Definition 10.3 (
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
).

For
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
, and
t\in[T]
, the function
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]
is defined recursively as follows: for each transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\begin{cases}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t},q_{t+1}),&t<T\\
\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\},&t=T\end{cases}

The following corollary follows directly from the definitions of
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}
and
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
:

Corollary 10.4.

For every
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in[T]
, and
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Lyu
(
24
)
shows the following two claims about
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}
:

Claim 10.5 (
(
24
)
).

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,
T\in\mathbb{N}
, and
q_{1}\in Q
, we have
\mathrm{Lower}_{1,T}^{b}(q_{1})\leq\delta
.

Claim 10.6 (
(
24
)
).

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[T]
,
13
13

13

In the original claim in
(
24
)
,
t
is assumed to be at most
T-1
, which is a typo.
and transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t})

\displaystyle-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t})\leq\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Claim
10.5
combined with the definition of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
imply:

Corollary 10.7.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
and
T\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathrm{L}_{0,T}^{b}()\leq\delta
.

In some part of the proof, Lyu
(
24
)
wrongly refers to Claim
10.6
, while the following lemma is required. For completeness, we prove this lemma and use it in the next section.

Lemma 10.8.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[T]\cup\{0\}
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Proof.

For
t=T
, we need to show that

\displaystyle\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}-e^{-\epsilon}\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

For short, we refer to the LHS and RHS as
A
and
B
, respectively.
There are three cases:

•

If
\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, then
A=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),

which equals
B
.

•

If
\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\geq e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, then,
A=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),

which is at most
B
as
e^{\epsilon}\geq e^{-\epsilon}
.

•

Otherwise, if
e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})<\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})<e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
,
A=0
is at most
B
since by the condition of the case distinction,
B=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
is positive.

For
t<T
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t})

\displaystyle=\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})-e^{-\epsilon}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\left(\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),

where the last inequality holds because for every
q_{t+1}\in Q
, by Claim
10.6

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})

\displaystyle-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{1-b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t+1})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

∎

10.1.1.
Control Function
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}=\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
(Definition).

This section introduces our main new technical contribution.
To remove the assumption of having an upper bound
T
on the number of queries, we introduce
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
as the limit of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
and show that it satisfies all properties needed by the proof of
(
24
)
.
Thus, differently from
(
24
)
, we will use
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}
and
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}
to define the function
\phi_{t}
.

Definition 10.9 (
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
).

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]
is defined as follows: for each transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})=\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}).

To ensure
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
is well-defined, we need to show that the limit of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
exists. We will upper bound the values of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
by
1
and show that this function is non-decreasing in
T
. As a result, for each
t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
, the limit
\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
exists.
We need the following lemma throughout the section:

Lemma 10.10.

For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
, transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}
, and query
q_{t}\in Q
, we have

\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t},a_{t}).

Proof.

For
a_{t}\in A
, let
\mu_{t}^{b}(a_{t}\mid q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1},q_{t})
denote the probability that
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
returns the answer
a_{t}
in response to
q_{t}
conditioned on
q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1}
being the history of previous queries and answers. By the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1})\cdot\mu_{t}^{b}(a_{t}\mid q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1})\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mu_{t}^{b}(a_{t}\mid q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},a_{1},\dots,q_{t-1},a_{t-1})

∎

To upper bound
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
, we first need to upper bound
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
:

Lemma 10.11.

For every
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in[T]
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1},q_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Proof.

Fix
T\geq t
. The proof is by induction on
t
from
T
to
1
. For
t=T
, by definition and Lemma
10.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}=\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

For
t<T
, by the inductive hypothesis,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})=\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),

completing the induction.
∎

The following lemma implies that the values of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
are upper bounded by
1
.

Lemma 10.12.

For every
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in[T]\cup\{0\}
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Proof.

For
t=T
, by definition and Lemma
10.10
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle=\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

\displaystyle\leq\max\{0,\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

For
t<T
, by Lemma
10.11
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t},q_{t+1})\leq\sup_{q_{t}\in Q}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

∎

To ensure the function
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
is well-defined, it remains to show that
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
is non-decreasing in
T
. To prove so, we first need to show:

Lemma 10.13.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}
is monotonically non-decreasing in
T
.

Proof.

We need to show that for every
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\leq T
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\leq\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}).

Fix
T\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. We show this by reverse induction on
t
: The base case is
t=T
. By Lemma
10.10
, for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{T,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T-1},q_{T})=\sum\limits_{a_{T}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{a_{T}\in A}\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\max\{0,\sum\limits_{a_{T+1}\in A}\mu_{T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T+1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{a_{T}\in A}\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\sum\limits_{a_{T+1}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T+1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})\}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{a_{T}\in A}\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{T+1,T+1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T},q_{T+1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lower}_{T,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T-1},q_{T})

For the induction step, assume that
t<T
and that for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t},q_{t+1})
, we have

\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t},q_{t+1})\leq\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t},q_{t+1}).

We need to show
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\leq\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
.
By definition,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{a_{t}\in A}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t},q_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{a_{t}\in A}\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t},q_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})

∎

Lemma 10.14.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the function
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}
is monotonically non-decreasing in
T
.

Proof.

We need to show that for every
T\in\mathbb{N}
,
t\in[T]\cup\{0\}
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\mathrm{L}_{t,T}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mathrm{L}_{t,T+1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

Fix
T\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
. We show this by reverse induction on
t
from
T
to
0
.

The base case is
t=T
. By Lemma
10.10
, for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{T,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})=\max\{0,\mu_{T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T})\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\max\{0,\sum\limits_{a_{T+1}\in A}\mu_{T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T+1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1}))\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\sum\limits_{a_{T+1}\in A}\max\{0,\mu_{T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})-e^{\epsilon}\mu_{T+1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T+1})\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{q_{T+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{T+1,T+1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T},q_{T+1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{T,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{T-1},q_{T},a_{T})

For the induction step, assume that
t<T
and that for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t+1})
, we have
\mathrm{L}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t+1})\leq\mathrm{L}_{t+1,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t+1})
. We need to show
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mathrm{L}_{t,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
.
By definition of
\mathrm{L}_{t,T+1}
and Lemma
10.13
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,q_{t},a_{t})

\displaystyle=\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t},q_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{q_{t+1}\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t+1,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t},q_{t+1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{L}_{t,T+1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,q_{t},a_{t})

∎

10.1.2.
Control Function
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}=\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}
(Properties).

In this section, we present the properties of
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
, required in the next sections. The definition of
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}
combined with Corollary
10.7
, Lemma
10.8
, and Lemma
10.12
gives the following corollaries, respectively:

Corollary 10.15.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, we have
\mathrm{L}_{0}^{b}()\leq\delta
.

Corollary 10.16.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle-e^{-\epsilon}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Lemma 10.17.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

To show the next property, we need the following known fact:

Lemma 10.18 (Monotone Convergence).

Let
f:\mathbb{N}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function such that
\sum_{m}f(m,1)>-\infty
and that is non-decreasing in the second parameter. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}f(m,n)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}f(m,n)

Lemma
10.18
combined with Lemma
10.14
implies:

Corollary 10.19.

For every
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
, and
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

Lemma 10.20.

For every
b\in\{0,1\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
transcript
(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}
, and query
q_{t}\in Q
,

\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})\geq\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})

Proof.

First, we will show that for every
T>t-1
,

\mathrm{L}_{t-1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})\geq\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})

Since
t-1\neq T
, by definition of
\mathrm{L}_{t-1,T}^{b}
and Corollary
10.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t-1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})

\displaystyle=\sup_{q\in Q}\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1},q)\geq\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1},q_{t})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t}).

Thus, by Corollary
10.19
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\geq t-1,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t-1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})=\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t-1,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t-1})

\displaystyle\geq\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\lim_{T\geq t,T\to\infty}\mathrm{L}_{t,T}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})=\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots a_{t})

∎

10.2.
Reduction.

To define
\mathcal{P}
and simulate
\mathcal{M}
, Lyu
(
24
)
constructs four families of probability mass functions (PMFs), corresponding to each outcome of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
. Intuitively, each family describes an IM and consists of PMFs over the answer set
A
for every
t\in[T]
and every history
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}\times Q
. These PMFs represent the probability of returning each answer in response to
q_{t}
, conditioned on
q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}
being the history of queries and answers. Similarly, we define
\mathcal{P}
based on four families of PMFs:
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,0)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,1)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
, corresponding to the four possible output sequences of
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
. We note that in our construction, each family includes a PMF over
A
for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every history
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
. Moreover, the definitions of these PMFs are not the same as the ones in
(
24
)
.

10.2.1.
Constructing
\mathcal{P}
.

In
(
24
)
, the post-processing mechanism initially interacts with
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
and stores its output
r=(\tau,c)\in\{\top,\bot\}\times\{0,1\}
. It then answers the adversary’s queries by itself using the family of PMFs corresponding to
r
. Our high-level idea is to construct the families
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,0)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,1)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
in such a way that, under certain conditions, knowledge of
\tau
alone suffices to choose the appropriate PMF. Recall that the IPM
\mathcal{P}
interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
instead of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
. We design
\mathcal{P}
to initially interact with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
and store its response.
\mathcal{P}
then interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
for the second time when it cannot choose a PMF using only the first response.

Specifically,
\mathcal{P}
has a single initial state
\left((),()\right)
, where
()
represents an empty sequence. We denote the state of
\mathcal{P}
by a pair
(r,h)
, where
r
is the sequence of responses from
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
, and
h
is the history of queries and answers exchanged with the adversary: right after receiving a message from
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
,
\mathcal{P}
appends it to
r
, and right before returning an answer
a
to a query
q
from the adversary,
\mathcal{P}
appends both
q
and
a
to
h
.

To generate a response
a
to query
q
,
\mathcal{P}
proceeds as follows: If
q
is the first query (i.e.,
r=()
), then
\mathcal{P}
initially interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
and appends its response to
r
. After this, whether
q
is the first query or not, the first time the following condition does
not
hold,
\mathcal{P}
interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
for the second time and appends its answer to
r
:
the PMFs corresponding to
(h,q)
in
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
are identical, and the PMFs corresponding to
(h,q)
in
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,1)}
are identical.
After that,
\mathcal{P}
never interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
again.

After the potential interactions with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
,
\mathcal{P}
generates the answer
a
as follows:

•

If
r\in\{\top,\bot\}\times\{0,1\}
, then
\mathcal{P}
samples
a
from the PMF corresponding to
(h,q)
in
\mathcal{F}_{r}
.

•

Otherwise, by design,
r\in\{\top,\bot\}
, and the PMFs in
\mathcal{F}_{(r,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(r,1)}
corresponding to
(h,q)
are identical. In this case,
\mathcal{P}
samples
a
from this common PMF.

10.2.2.
Constructing
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,b)}
s.

Using the control function
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
, Lyu
(
24
)
introduces three conditions and constructs a sequence of functions
\phi_{t}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]^{2}
for
t\in\{1,\dots,T\}
that satisfy these conditions. The families of PMFs are defined then based on these functions. We remove the dependence on the bound
T
by replacing
\mathrm{Lower}_{t,T}
with
\mathrm{L}_{t}
in the conditions, and extend the construction of
\phi_{t}
to all
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, we introduce an additional condition to those given in
(
24
)
, which we will later use to bound the number of times
\mathcal{P}
interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
.

Let
\phi_{t}^{0}
and
\phi_{t}^{1}
denote the functions that return the first and second components, respectively, of the output of
\phi_{t}
. Intuitively, each
\phi_{t}^{b}
represents an IM: for every query sequence
q_{1},\dots,q_{t}
, it assigns a probability to each answer sequence
a_{1},\dots,a_{t}
. That is, for any query sequence
q_{1},\dots,q_{t}
, the function
\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\cdot,q_{2},\cdot,\dots,q_{t},\cdot)
defines a PMF over
A^{t}
.

For
t=0
, we define
\phi_{t}()=(1,1)
. We defer the detailed construction of the functions
\phi_{t}
to Section
10.3
. However, we assume the existence of
\phi_{t}
satisfying the following conditions for each
b\in\{0,1\}
:

(I)

For every transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\geq\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

(II)

For every transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,

\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\delta\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

(III)

For every transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
,

\sum_{a\in A}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)=\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

(IV)

For each query sequence
(q_{1},\dots,q_{t})\in Q^{t}
, if
\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
, then

\phi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\phi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\geq\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

for every
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the family
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,b)}
consists of functions
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}:A\to[0,1]
defined for every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{*}\times Q
, where
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}
is defined as follows:

•

If
\phi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})>0
, then for each answer
a_{t}\in A
,

f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})=\frac{\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})}{\phi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})}.

•

Otherwise, if
\phi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})=0
,
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})=0
for all
a_{t}\in A
, except for one fixed arbitrary answer
a^{*}\in A
, for which
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a^{*})=1
. This case is included only for formality, as the corresponding PMFs are never used by
\mathcal{P}
.

Since
\phi_{t-1}
and
\phi_{t}
are non-negative,
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})
is also non-negative. Furthermore, by
(III)
, we have
\sum_{a_{t}\in A}f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a)=1
, implying that
f_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})
is a PMF.

10.2.3.
Constructing
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,b)}
s.

Similar to the previous section,
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
are defined based on a sequence of functions
\psi_{t}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]^{2}
for each
t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. For
t=0
,
\psi_{t}()=(1,1)
. For
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\psi_{t}
as follows:

•

If
\delta=1
, then
\psi_{t}
is identical to
\phi_{t}
.

•

Otherwise, for each
b\in\{0,1\}
and
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
,
\psi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
equals

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-\delta)(e^{\epsilon}-1)}\Big(e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\delta e^{\epsilon}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\delta\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\Big).

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the family
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,b)}
consists of functions
g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}:A\to[0,1]
for each
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{*}\times Q
, where
g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}
is defined as follows:

•

If
\psi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})>0
, then for each answer
a_{t}\in A
,

g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})=\frac{\psi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})}{\psi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})}.

•

Otherwise,
g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a_{t})=0
for all
a_{t}\in A
, except for one fixed arbitrary answer
a^{*}\in A
, for which
g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}(a^{*})=1
.

By condition
(II)
,
\psi_{t}^{b}
is non-negative. Also, by Lemma
10.10
and condition
(III)
,

\sum_{a\in A}\psi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)=\psi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),

implying that
g_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}}^{b}
is a PMF.

10.2.4.
Proof of Lemma
5.6
.

By definition of
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
and
\mathcal{P}
, for every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t}
, the probability of
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
returning the answers
a_{1},\dots,a_{t}
to the queries
q_{1},\dots,q_{t}
is

\displaystyle\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+(1-\delta)\frac{e^{\epsilon}}{1+e^{\epsilon}}\psi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+(1-\delta)\frac{1}{1+e^{\epsilon}}\psi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

By the definition of
\psi_{t}
, this probability equals

\displaystyle\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\frac{e^{\epsilon}}{(1+e^{\epsilon})(e^{\epsilon}-1)}\Big(e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle\quad-\delta e^{\epsilon}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\delta\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{(1+e^{\epsilon})(e^{\epsilon}-1)}\Big(e^{\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\delta e^{\epsilon}\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Thus, the views of any adversary interacting with
\mathcal{M}(s_{b})
and
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}(b)
are identically distributed, implying that these mechanisms are equivalent.

Furthermore, for every query sequence
(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})
, if the answer distributions of
\mathcal{M}(s_{0})
and
\mathcal{M}(s_{1})
to
(q_{1},\dots,q_{k})
are identical, then by Lemma
10.10
, for every
t\leq k
and every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
, we have

\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Therefore, by condition
(IV)
, for every
t\leq k
and every
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
,

\phi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\phi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

Consequently,

\psi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\psi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

By the definitions of
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
,
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,0)}
, and
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,1)}
, this implies that for every
t\leq k
and answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})
, the PMF associated with
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
is identical in both
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(\bot,1)}
, and likewise in
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,0)}
and
\mathcal{F}_{(\top,1)}
. Hence, by the construction of
\mathcal{P}
, this mechanism interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
only once. Moreover, by design,
\mathcal{P}
never interacts with
\mathrm{IRR}_{\epsilon,\delta}
more than twice.

10.3.
Construction of
\phi_{t}

For each
q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t}
, Lyu
(
24
)

initializes the values of
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
with the ones from
\mathrm{L}_{t,T}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
.
(Removing the upper bound assumption, they are initialized by the values of
\mathrm{L}_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
.) Then, they iterate over all possible answers
a_{t}\in A
in a fixed order and gradually increase the value of
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
to satisfy conditions
(II)
and
(III)
, while ensuring that condition
(I)
remains valid. This iterative procedure requires
A
to be finite; otherwise, the construction algorithm does not terminate. Moreover, the fixed iteration order can lead to an imbalance in which earlier values grow significantly larger than those that come later in the order. In our new construction, we control and balance this increase.

Intuitively, we introduce a temporary upper bound and increase the values of the function
\mathrm{L}_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
to reach the minimum of the original upper bounds and the new temporary ones. This results in an intermediate function, denoted by
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
. Then, we remove the temporary upper bound and continue increasing the values of
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
(if needed) to meet the original upper bounds. The final function is the desired
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
. We will show that if
\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
, then the functions
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
and
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
are identical. We set the temporary upper bound in a way that this implies the condition
(IV)
holds.

Formally, we inductively construct
\phi_{t}
for each
t\in\mathbb{N}
, assuming that
\phi_{t-1}
exists and satisfies conditions
(I)
,
(II)
and
(IV)
. We then show that
\phi_{t}
satisfies all conditions from
(I)
to
(IV)
. For the base case
t=0
, we define
\phi_{0}()=(1,1)
. By Corollary
10.15
, this choice satisfies condition
(I)
. Moreover, for
\phi_{0}^{0}()=\phi_{0}^{1}()=1
and
\mu_{0}^{0}()=\mu_{0}^{1}()=1
, condition
(II)
is
\delta\cdot 1-e^{-\epsilon}\cdot\delta\cdot 1\leq 1-e^{-\epsilon}\cdot 1
, which is true because
\delta\leq 1
. Furthermore, since
\phi_{0}^{0}()=\phi_{0}^{1}()=1\geq\min\{1,\delta+\delta\}
, condition
(IV)
is also satisfied.

Remark 10.21.

In the rest of this section, we assume
\phi_{t-1}
exists and satisfies conditions
(I)
,
(II)
and
(IV)
.

Section
10.3.1
outlines the preliminaries required for defining
\xi_{t}
and
\phi_{t}
. In Section
10.3.2
, we define the intermediate function
\xi_{t}
. Section
10.3.3
presents the new construction of
\phi_{t}
, and finally, in Section
10.3.4
, we prove that
\phi_{t}
satisfies conditions
(I)
to
(IV)
.

10.3.1.
Preliminaries.

In this section, we introduce the fundamental concepts and notations needed for defining
\phi_{t}
when
A
is not finite.

Definition 10.22 (Well-Order).

A well-order on a set
X
is a total order (i.e., for every
x,y\in X
, either
x<y
,
x>y
, or
x=y
) such that every non-empty subset of
X
has a least element.

Since
A
is countable,
A
can be injected into
\mathbb{N}
. Listing elements of
A
in the order given by their images under this injection gives a well-order
<_{A}
on
A
. Note that for any non-empty subset
B\subseteq A
, the image of
B
in
\mathbb{N}
has a least element, whose preimage is the least element of
B
under
<_{A}
. We will later use the following remark when constructing
\phi_{t}
.

Remark 10.23.

By the definition of
<_{A}
, each element of
A
is either the least element of
A
or has an immediate predecessor in
A
.

Definition 10.24 (
\Gamma_{<a}
and
\Gamma_{>a}
).

For any answer
a\in A
,
\Gamma_{<a}=\{a^{\prime}\in A\mid a^{\prime}<_{A}a\}
and
\Gamma_{>a}=\{a^{\prime}\in A\mid a<_{A}a^{\prime}\}
.

Lemma 10.25 (Transfinite Recursion
(
32
)
).

Let
f
be a function recursively defined over
A
according to the well-order
<_{A}
. If for each answer
a\in A
, the value of
f(a)
is uniquely determined by the values
f(a^{\prime})
for every
a^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{<a}
, then
f
exists and is unique.

In the next section, we construct functions
f:A\to[0,1]^{2}
and apply Lemma
10.25
to prove that they are well-defined. To apply this lemma, we must ensure that each value
f(a)
is uniquely determined based on the previously defined values
f(a^{\prime})
for all
a^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{<a}
. Thus, to define
f(a)
, we first select a subset
\mathcal{C}\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
of desired values satisfying certain properties derived from the values of
f(a^{\prime})
for
a^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{<a}
. We then set
f(a)
to the lexicographically maximal element of
\mathcal{C}
. The following definitions and lemma formalizes this and guarantee that
f(a)
is uniquely defined in this way.

Definition 10.26 (Maximal Pair).

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq[0,1]^{2}
. A pair
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
is said to be
maximal
in
\mathcal{C}
if there does not exist a pair
(y_{0},y_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
such that
(x_{0},x_{1})\neq(y_{0},y_{1})
,
x_{0}\leq y_{0}
, and
x_{1}\leq y_{1}
.

Definition 10.27 (Lexicographic Order
<_{lex}
).

The relation
<_{lex}
is a binary ordering on
[0,1]^{2}
defined as follows: for any
(r_{1},r_{2}),(r_{3},r_{4})\in[0,1]^{2}
, we have
(r_{1},r_{2})<_{lex}(r_{3},r_{4})
if and only if
r_{1}\leq r_{3}
, or
r_{1}=r_{3}
and
r_{2}\leq r_{4}
.

Lemma 10.28.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a non-empty compact subset of
[0,1]^{2}
.

(a)

(x_{0},x_{1})<_{lex}(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
for all
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
.

(b)

(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
is maximal in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

(a): Define
x_{0}^{*}=\sup\{x_{0}\mid(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}\}
. Since
\mathcal{C}
is non-empty, the set
\{x_{0}\mid(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}\}
is non-empty. Also, by definition,
x_{0}^{*}\leq 1
. As we are taking a continuous projection
(x_{0},x_{1})\to x_{0}
of a compact set
\mathcal{C}
, this supremum is actually a maximum, so there exists
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
such that
x_{0}=x_{0}^{*}
.

Define
x_{1}^{*}=\sup\{x_{1}\mid(x_{0}^{*},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}\}
. Again, the set
\{x_{1}\mid(x_{0}^{*},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}\}
is non-empty since there exists
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
with
x_{0}=x_{0}^{*}
. By the same compactness argument, this supremum is also attained. Hence,
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})\in\mathcal{C}
. By definition,
(x_{0},x_{1})<_{lex}(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
for every
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
, which trivially implies that
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
is the only pair in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying this property.

(b): Let
(x_{0},x_{1})
be a pair in
\mathcal{C}
satisfying
x_{0}\geq x_{0}^{*}
and
x_{1}\geq x_{1}^{*}
. Since
x_{0}\geq x_{0}^{*}
and
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
, by definition of
x_{0}^{*}
, we have
x_{0}^{*}=x_{0}
. Since
x_{1}\geq x_{1}^{*}
,
x_{0}^{*}=x_{0}
, and
(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathcal{C}
, by definition of
x_{1}^{*}
, we have
x_{1}^{*}=x_{1}
. Therefore,
(x_{0},x_{1})=(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
, implying that
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
is maximal.
∎

10.3.2.
Defining
\xi_{t}
and showing its existence.

To define the function
\xi_{t}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]^{2}
, we fix
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
and recursively define
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
for each answer
a_{t}\in A
according to the order
<_{A}
. We define
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
, where
x_{0}^{*}
and
x_{1}^{*}
are uniquely chosen from the maximal feasible solutions of a constraint satisfaction problem with two variables
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
. The constraints for this problem involve the known functions
\mu_{t}
,
\phi_{t-1}
,
\mathrm{L}_{t}
, and the known values
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)
for every
a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}
.

Consider the following constraints for the variables
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
:

(
\xi
.1)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

x_{b}\geq\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

(
\xi
.2)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}}

\displaystyle\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+x_{b}+\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{>a_{t}}}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

(
\xi
.3)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle x_{b}-e^{-\epsilon}x_{1-b}\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

(
\xi
.4)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle x_{b}\leq\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

Let the set
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
denote the set of feasible solutions
(x_{0},x_{1})
that satisfy all the constraints above. Each constraint is a non-strict inequality of the form
h_{i}(x_{0},x_{1})\geq 0
, where each
h_{i}
is a continuous function. Since the solution set of a single non-strict inequality defined by a continuous function is closed, and the intersection of finitely many closed sets is also closed, the set
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is closed. Furthermore, as it is a subset of the compact set
[0,1]^{2}
, it is itself compact. If
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is nonempty, then by Lemma
10.28
, the lexicographically maximum pair
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
of
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
exists, belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
, and is maximal in
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
. We define
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
.

Claim 10.29.

The function
\xi_{t}
is well-defined and exists.

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps: (i) proving that
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is non-empty, and (ii) applying the transfinite recursion theorem (Lemma
10.25
) and concluding that
\xi_{t}
exists.

(i):

Fix any
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
. By Remark
10.23
, each answer in
A
is either the smallest element of
A
or has an immediate predecessor. We will inductively show that for every
a_{t}\in A
, the values
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
and
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
satisfy the constraints for defining
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, and consequently,
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is not empty.

Trivially,
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
satisfies Constraint
(

ξ

.1)
. Moreover, by Corollary
10.16
, it satisfies Constraint
(

ξ

.3)
. To prove Constraint
(

ξ

.2)
is satisfied, we need to show that

(2)

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{>a_{t}}\cup\{a_{t}\}}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),

We prove this by induction on
a_{t}\in A
:

Base Case:

Suppose
a_{t}
is the least element of
A
. Then,
\Gamma_{<a_{t}}
is empty, reducing our goal to showing that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\end{subarray}}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

We know that
\phi_{t-1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})
satisfies condition
(I)
. (see Remark
10.21
.) Thus, by Lemma
10.20
,

\displaystyle\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\end{subarray}}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),

finishing the proof for the base case.

Inductive Step:

Let
a^{*}
denote the immediate predecessor of
a_{t}
. By inductive hypothesis,
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a^{*})
exists and satisfies Constraint
(

ξ

.3)
. Since
\Gamma_{<a_{t}}=\Gamma_{<a^{*}}\cup\{a^{*}\}
, Constraints
(

ξ

.2)
for defining
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a^{*})
is identical to (
2
). Thus, by inductive hypothesis, inequality (
2
) holds, completing the induction.

It remains to prove that Constraint
(

ξ

.4)
is satisfied. By Lemma
10.17
,
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
. Also, as
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1-b}
is non-negative,
\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
. Thus,

\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\},

and Constraint
(

ξ

.4)
is satisfied.

(ii):

For every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
, the values of the function
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
is uniquely determined by the preceding values of this function in the well-order
<_{A}
. Thus, by the transfinite recursion theorem, the function
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
exists and is uniquely defined. Consequently, the function
\xi_{t}
exists and is uniquely defined.
∎

10.3.3.
Defining
\phi_{t}
and showing its existence.

To define the function
\phi_{t}:(Q\times A)^{t}\to[0,1]^{2}
, we fix
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
and recursively define
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
for each answer
a_{t}\in A
based on the
<_{A}
order. Similar to the definition of
\xi_{t}
, for each
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, we determine
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
using a set of constraints. These constraints involve the known functions
\xi_{t}
,
\mu_{t}
,
\phi_{t-1}
,
\mathrm{L}_{t}
, and the known values
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)
for every
a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}
.

Consider the following set of constraints for the variables
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
:

(
\phi
.1)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\delta x_{b}\geq\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}).

(
\phi
.2)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}}

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+x_{b}+\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{>a_{t}}}{\frac{1}{\delta}}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

(
\phi
.3)

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\delta x_{b}-e^{-\epsilon}\delta x_{1-b}\leq\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

Let
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
denote the set of feasible solutions
(x_{0},x_{1})
satisfying the constraints above. As in Section
10.3.2
, one can show that
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is compact. Therefore, if
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is nonempty, then by Lemma
10.28
, the lexicographically maximum pair
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
of
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
exists, belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
, and is maximal in
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
.

Define
\mathcal{G}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
to be the set of all maximal elements in
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
. By the definition of maximality, this set either contains no pair of the form
(z,z)
with identical components, or it contains exactly one such pair, denoted
(z^{*},z^{*})
. We define
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=(z^{*},z^{*})
if there exists
(z^{*},z^{*})\in\mathcal{G}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
and
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
otherwise. In either case,
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
is a maximal solution for conditions
(

ϕ

.1)
to
(

ϕ

.3)
.

Claim 10.30.

The function
\phi_{t}
is well-defined and exists.

Proof.

The proof consists of two steps: (i) proving that
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is non-empty, and (ii) applying the transfinite recursion theorem (Lemma
10.25
) and concluding that
\phi_{t}
exists.

(i):

Fix any
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
. By Remark
10.23
, each answer in
A
is either the smallest element of
A
or has an immediate predecessor. We will inductively show that for every
a_{t}\in A
, the values
\frac{1}{\delta}\xi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
and
\frac{1}{\delta}\xi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
satisfy all the constraints for the optimization problem defining
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, and consequently,
\mathcal{C}_{q_{1},\dots,a_{t}}
is not empty.

First, note that
\frac{1}{\delta}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
trivially satisfies Constraint
(

ϕ

.1)
. Moreover, by Constraint
(

ξ

.3)
, it satisfies Constraint
(

ϕ

.3)
. To prove Constraint
(

ϕ

.2)
is satisfied, it suffices to show that

(3)

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)

\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{>a_{t}}\cup\{a_{t}\}}\frac{1}{\delta}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),

We prove this by induction on
a_{t}\in A
:

Base Case:

Suppose
a_{t}
is the least element of
A
. Then,
\Gamma_{<a_{t}}
is empty, reducing our goal to showing that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in A\end{subarray}}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Let
a+t=a_{1}^{\prime}<_{A}a_{2}^{\prime}<_{A}\dots
denote the ordered elements of
A
. As
\mathrm{L}_{t}
is non-negative, by Constraint
(

ξ

.2)
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in\Gamma_{<a_{n}^{\prime}}\cup\{a_{n}^{\prime}\}\end{subarray}}\xi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Taking
\lim_{n\to\infty}
when
A
is infinite or setting
n=|A|
when
A
is finite gives inequality (
3
).

Inductive Step:

Let
a^{*}
be the immediate predecessor of
a_{t}
. Since
\Gamma_{<a_{t}}=\Gamma_{<a^{*}}\cup\{a^{*}\}
, Constraints
(

ϕ

.2)
for defining
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a^{*})
is identical to (
3
). By inductive hypothesis,
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a^{*})
exists and satisfies
(

ϕ

.2)
. Thus, inequality (
3
) holds, completing the induction.

(ii):

For every
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
, the values of the function
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
is uniquely determined by the preceding values of this function in the well-order
<_{A}
. Thus, by the transfinite recursion theorem, the function
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},\cdot)
exists and is uniquely defined. Consequently, the function
\phi_{t}
exists and is uniquely defined.
∎

10.3.4.
Satisfying Conditions
(I)
to
(IV)

Our objective was to construct
\phi_{t}^{b}
so that it fulfills conditions
(I)
to
(IV)
. Combination of Constraints
(

ϕ

.1)
and
(

ξ

.1)
gives condition
(I)
. Moreover, Constraint
(

ϕ

.3)
directly implies condition
(II)
. The proof of the following claim is identical to a proof in
(
24
)
:

Claim 10.31.

\phi_{t}
satisfies condition
(III)
.

Proof.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
and every transcript
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
, by Constraint
(

ϕ

.2)
and non-negativity of
\xi^{b}
, for every
a_{t}
,

\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}\cup\{a_{t}\}}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),

which in both cases where
A
is finite and countable implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

For the sake of contradiction, suppose there exists
b\in\{0,1\}
and
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})
such that

(4)

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)<\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Fix
b
. As
\phi_{t}^{b}
is lower bounded by
\xi_{t}^{b}/\delta
and the values of
\phi_{t}
are maximal solutions, this strict inequality implies that for every
a_{t}\in A
, Constraint
(

ϕ

.2)
for
b
is not tight. Consequently, for every
a_{t}\in A
, we must have had

\displaystyle\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\delta\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

otherwise, the value of
\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
could have increased, which contradicts the maximality.
Taking a sum over all
a_{t}\in A
, we get

(5)

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\delta\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-e^{-\epsilon}\delta\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})-e^{-\epsilon}\mu_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

By
(II)
and Remark
10.21
, we know that the RHS is at least

\delta\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})-e^{-\epsilon}\delta\phi_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

Plugging this to (
5
) and reformulating it, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle-\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\geq e^{-\epsilon}(\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\phi_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}))

By (
4
), the LHS is negative, implying that the RHS is negative too.
Thus,
\sum_{a\in A}\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)<\phi_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})
. Hence, by an identical argument, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\phi_{t}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

\displaystyle-\phi_{t-1}^{1-b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\geq e^{-\epsilon}(\sum_{a_{t}\in A}\phi_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})-\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})),

which is a contradiction when
\epsilon>0
, implying that condition
(III)
holds.
∎

To show
\phi_{t}
satisfies conditions
(IV)
, we need the following lemma:

Lemma 10.32.

For any query sequence
(q_{1},\dots,q_{t})\in Q^{t}
, if
\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
, then

\xi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\xi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
.

Proof.

Fix
(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t})\in(Q\times A)^{t-1}\times Q
. We will inductively show that for every
a_{t}\in A
,

\xi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\xi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}.

Recall
\xi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
is defined as the lexicographically maximum feasible solution
(x_{0}^{*},x_{1}^{*})
. By Constraint
(

ξ

.4)
, for each
b\in\{0,1\}
, we have

x_{b}^{*}\leq\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}.

Therefore, to prove the lemma, it suffices to show

x_{0}=x_{1}=\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

is a feasible solution:

•

(

ξ

.1)
: By Lemma
10.17
and non-negativity of
\xi_{t}
, this constraint is satisfied.

•

(

ξ

.2)
: To show Constraint
(

ξ

.2)
is satisfied, we need to show for each
b\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{<a_{t}}\cup\{a_{t}\}}

\displaystyle\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\}

\displaystyle+\sum_{a\in\Gamma_{>a_{t}}}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

Since

\mathrm{L}_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\leq\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\},

it suffices to prove

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in A}

\displaystyle\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\}\leq\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})

By condition
(IV)
and Remark
10.21
,

\displaystyle\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\geq\min\{\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})+\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\}

Therefore, by Lemma
10.10
and Lemma
10.20
,

\displaystyle\phi_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\geq\min\{\mu_{t-1}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}),\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})+\mathrm{L}_{t-1}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})\}

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\sum_{a\in A}\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),\sum_{a\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+\sum_{a\in A}\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\right\}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{a\in A}\min\{\mu_{t}^{b}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1},q_{t},a)\},

completing the proof.

•

(

ξ

.3)
: Recall that
\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
. To prove this constraint is satisfied, we need to show

(1-e^{-\epsilon})\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}\leq(1-e^{-\epsilon})\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),

which is trivially true.

•

(

ξ

.4)
: This constraint is trivially satisfied as equality holds.

∎

Claim 10.33.

\phi_{t}
satisfies conditions
(IV)
.

Proof.

For any query sequence
(q_{1},\dots,q_{t})\in Q^{t}
, if

(
\star
)

\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\mu_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})

for every answer sequence
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
, then by Lemma
10.32
,

(
\star\star
)

\xi_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\xi_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}

for all
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
.
Moreover, by Lemma
10.10
and

⋆

‣
10.3.4
,
\mu_{t-1}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})=\mu_{t-1}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})
for all
(a_{1},\dots,a_{t})\in A^{t}
. Thus, by Remark
10.21
and condition
(IV)
,

(
\star\star\star
)

\phi_{t-1}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1})=\phi_{t-1}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t-1}).

Therefore, by equations (

⋆

‣
10.3.4
), (

⋆
⁣
⋆

‣
10.3.4
) and (

⋆
⋆
⋆

‣
10.3.4
), Constraints
(

ϕ

.1)
,
(

ϕ

.2)
, and
(

ϕ

.3)
are symmetric for
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
. Hence, there exists a maximal feasible solution
(z^{*},z^{*})
satisfying all the constraints. By definition of
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})
, we have
\phi_{t}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})=(z^{*},z^{*})
. Also, by Constraint
(

ϕ

.1)
,
z^{*}
is at least
\min\{\mu_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t}),\mathrm{L}_{t}^{0}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})+\mathrm{L}_{t}^{1}(q_{1},\dots,a_{t})\}
.

∎

11.
f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}
-concurrent composition

In this section, we prove:

Lemma 0 (Restatement of Lemma
5.9
).

For every
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, the
f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}
-concurrent composition of continual mechanisms is
(0,\delta)
-differentially private.

In Section
4
, we assumed that all composed CMs have discrete answer distributions. Accordingly, we also prove Lemma
5.9
under this assumption. During this section, we assume the domain of all variables is finite or countable. For every random variable
Y
, we denote its support by
\mathrm{supp}(Y)
.

We begin by proving some intermediate results needed for the final proof. Vadhan et al.
(
34
)
show that to guarantee an IM is differentially private, it suffices to consider only deterministic adversaries. Specifically, the following holds:

Lemma 11.1 (
(
34
)
).

An IM
\mathcal{M}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. a neighboring relation
\sim
if and only if for every deterministic adversary
\mathcal{A}
and every pair of neighboring initial states
s\sim s^{\prime}
, the random variables
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{M}(s))
and
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{M}(s^{\prime}))
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable.

By Definition
3.7
, this result extends naturally to CMs:

Corollary 11.2.

A CM
\mathcal{M}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. a verification function
f
if and only if for every deterministic adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the random variables
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
and
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
are
(\epsilon,\delta)
-indistinguishable.

The definition of
(0,\delta)
-DP CMs can be equivalently expressed using the notion of
total variation
:

Definition 11.3 (Total Variation).

Let
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
be two variables with the same domain
\mathcal{Y}
. The
total variation distance
between
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
, denoted
\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})
, is defined as

\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})=\sup_{S\subseteq\mathcal{Y}}\Pr[Y^{0}\in S]-\Pr[Y^{1}\in S].

Lemma 11.4.

Let
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
be two random variables over the same domain
\mathcal{Y}
. For every
0\leq\delta\leq 1
,
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable if and only if
\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})\leq\delta
.

Proof.

By definition,
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable if and only if for every
S\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
,
\Pr[Y^{0}\in S]-\Pr[Y^{1}\in S]\leq\delta
. Therefore,
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable if and only if
\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})=\sup_{S\subseteq\mathcal{Y}}\Pr[Y^{0}\in S]-\Pr[Y^{1}\in S]\leq\delta
.
∎

Lemma 11.5.

Let
\Sigma
be a (discrete) set, and let
V^{0}
and
V^{1}
be random variables over
\Sigma^{*}
, i.e., finite-length sequences over
\Sigma
that terminate with probability
1
. Let
\$
be a symbol not in
\Sigma
. For
b\in\{0,1\}
, define the infinite sequence
W^{b}=(W_{1}^{b},W_{2}^{b},\dots)
by padding
V^{b}
with an infinite sequence of
\$
symbols. Suppose that for some
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\forall k>0,\quad\mathrm{TV}\left((W_{1}^{0},\dots,W_{k}^{0}),(W_{1}^{1},\dots,W_{k}^{1})\right)\leq\delta.

Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}(V^{0},V^{1})\leq\delta.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}(V^{0},V^{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{*}}\big|\Pr[V^{0}=\sigma]-\Pr[V^{1}=\sigma]\big|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma^{*}}\big|\Pr[W^{0}=\sigma\cdot\$^{\omega}]-\Pr[W^{1}=\sigma\cdot\$^{\omega}]\big|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{*}\cdot\$^{\omega}}\big|\Pr[W^{0}=\sigma^{\prime}]-\Pr[W^{1}=\sigma^{\prime}]\big|=\mathrm{TV}(W^{0},W^{1})

Thus, we need to show
\mathrm{TV}(W^{0},W^{1})\leq\delta
.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
W_{1:i}^{b}=(W_{1}^{b},\dots,W_{i}^{b})
denote the prefix of length
i
, and let
W_{i:}^{b}=(W_{i}^{b},W_{i+1}^{b},\dots)
denote the infinite suffix of
W^{b}
. Define:

\displaystyle T=\mathrm{TV}(W^{0},W^{1}),\quad T_{i}=\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:i}^{0},W_{1:i}^{1}),\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha(i)=\Pr[W_{i}^{0}\neq\$]+\Pr[W_{i}^{1}\neq\$].

We will show that

(1)

T_{i}
is non-decreasing in
i
.

(2)

For all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
T-T_{i}\leq\alpha(i+1)
.

Since each
T_{i}\leq 1
and the sequence is non-decreasing, the limit
T^{*}=\lim_{i\to\infty}T_{i}
exists. Moreover, since
W_{i}^{0}=\$
if and only if
|V^{b}|<i
and
V^{b}
is finite with probability
1
, the probability
\Pr[W_{i}^{b}\neq\$]
converges to zero as
i\to\infty
. Therefore,

T-T^{*}=\lim_{i\to\infty}T-T_{i}\leq\lim_{i\to\infty}\alpha(i)=0.

Since
T_{i}\leq\delta
for all
i
, we conclude that
T^{*}\leq\delta
. Thus,
T=\mathrm{TV}(W^{0},W^{1})\leq\delta
.

It remains to prove (1) and (2). Since
W_{1:i}^{0}
and
W_{1:i}^{1}
in
T_{i}=\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:i}^{0},W_{1:i}^{1})
equal
W_{1:i+1}^{0}
and
W_{1:i+1}^{1}
in
T_{i}=\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:i+1}^{0},W_{1:i+1}^{1})
with their last entries removed, the data-processing inequality implies
T_{i}\leq T_{i+1}
.
For (2),
define
X_{i}^{b}=W_{1:i}^{b}\$^{\omega}
for each
b\in\{0,1\}
. By definition,
T_{i}=\mathrm{TV}(X_{i}^{0},X_{i}^{1})
. We have

\displaystyle T-T_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{TV}(W^{0},W^{1})-\mathrm{TV}(X_{i}^{0},X_{i}^{1})

\displaystyle=\sup_{S}\left(\Pr[W^{0}\in S]-\Pr[W^{1}\in S]\right)-\sup_{S}\left(\Pr[X^{0}\in S]-\Pr[X^{1}\in S]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{S}\left(\Pr[W^{0}\in S]-\Pr[W^{1}\in S]-\Pr[X^{0}\in S]+\Pr[X^{1}\in S]\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{S}\left(\Pr[W^{0}\in S]-\Pr[X^{0}\in S]\right)+\sup_{S}\left(\Pr[X^{1}\in S]-\Pr[W^{1}\in S]\right)

\displaystyle=\Pr[W^{0}\neq X_{i}^{0}]+\Pr[W^{1}\neq X_{i}^{1}]

Since once an entry of
W^{b}
equals
\$
all subsequent entries remain
\$
, by definition of
X_{i}^{b}
, we have
W^{b}\neq X_{i}^{b}
if and only if
W_{i+1}^{b}\neq\$
. Therefore,

\displaystyle T-T_{i}\leq\Pr[W_{i+1}^{0}\neq\$]+\Pr[W_{i+1}^{1}\neq\$]=\alpha(i+1),

finishing the proof.
∎

Lemma 11.6 (The Maximal Coupling Lemma).

Let
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
be random variables with domain
\mathcal{Y}
. There exists a joint distribution for a pair of random variables
(Z^{0},Z^{1})
on
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{Y}
, called a
coupling
, such that:

(i)

For every
b\in\{0,1\}
, the marginal distribution of
Z^{b}
is identical to the distribution of
Y^{b}
.

(ii)

Let
S^{0}=\mathrm{supp}(Y^{0})
and
S^{1}=\mathrm{supp}(Y^{1})
. The probability of the event
Z^{0}=Z^{1}
is maximized and satisfies

\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}]=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min\left\{\Pr[Z^{0}=y],\Pr[Z^{1}=y]\right\}=\sum_{y\in S^{0}\cap S^{1}}\min\left\{\Pr[Z^{0}=y],\Pr[Z^{1}=y]\right\}.

Lemma 11.7 (The Coupling Lemma).

Let
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
be random variables with domain
\mathcal{Y}
. For every (not necessarily maximal) coupling
(Z^{0},Z^{1})
of
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
, where the marginals
Z^{0}
and
Z^{1}
are distributed as
Y^{0}
and
Y^{1}
, we have

\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})\leq\Pr[Z^{0}\neq Z^{1}].

Lemma 11.8.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
Y^{0}=(Y_{1}^{0},\dots,Y_{k}^{0})
and
Y^{1}=(Y_{1}^{1},\dots,Y_{k}^{1})
be random variables. For
i\in[k-1]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, set
S_{i}^{b}=\mathrm{supp}(W_{1:i}^{b})
and
S_{i}=S_{i}^{0}\cap S_{i}^{1}
, with
S_{0}=S_{0}^{b}=\emptyset
. For
i\in[k]
, let
\Delta_{i}:S_{i-1}\to[0,1]
be a function, and fix
\gamma\in[0,1]
. Suppose the following conditions hold:

•

For every
i\in[k]
and every
y_{1:i-1}\in S_{i-1}
,

\mathrm{TV}\left((Y_{i}^{0}|Y_{1:i-1}^{0}=y_{1:i-1}),(Y_{i}^{1}|Y_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1})\right)\leq\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1}).

•

For every
y_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}
,
1-\prod_{j=1}^{i}\left(1-\Delta_{j}(w_{1:j})\right)\leq\gamma
.

Then,

\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})\leq\gamma.

Proof.

We construct a coupling
\left(Z^{0}=(Z_{1}^{0},\dots,Z_{k}^{0}),Z^{1}=(Z_{1}^{1},\dots,Z_{k}^{1})\right)
for
(Y^{0},Y^{1})
and show that
\Pr[Z^{0}\neq Z^{1}]\leq\gamma
. By Lemma
11.7
, this implies
\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})\leq\gamma
. Throughout the proof, let
\mathcal{Y}
denote the union of all possible outcomes of
Y_{i}^{b}
over
i\in[k]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
.

Define
(Z_{1}^{0},Z_{1}^{1})
to be the maximal coupling of
Y_{1}^{0}
and
Y_{1}^{1}
given by Lemma
11.6
. For
i=2
to
k
, given samples
(Z_{1:i-1}^{0},Z_{1:i-1}^{1})
, we define
(Z_{i}^{0},Z_{i}^{1})
as follows:

•

If the histories are identical, i.e.
Z_{1:i-1}^{0}=Z_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1}
, then
(Z_{i}^{0},Z_{i}^{1})
is drawn from the maximal coupling of teh distributions of
{Y_{i}^{0}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{0}=y_{1:i-1}}
and
{Y_{i}^{1}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1}}
, given by Lemma
11.6
.

•

Otherwise, if
Z_{1:i-1}^{0}=y_{1:i-1}^{0}
and
Z_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1}^{1}
differ, then
Z_{i}^{0}
and
Z_{i}^{1}
are drawn independently from their respective conditional distributions
{Y_{i}^{0}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{0}=y_{1:i-1}^{0}}
and
{Y_{i}^{1}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1}^{1}}
.

By the chain rule, the probability
\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}]=\sum_{y_{1:k}\in S_{k}}\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}\!=\!y_{1:k}]
equals

\displaystyle\sum_{y_{1:k}\in S_{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\Pr\!\left[Z_{i}^{0}\!=\!Z_{i}^{1}\!=\!y_{i}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:i-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:i-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:i-1}\right]=

\displaystyle\sum_{y_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\Pr\!\left[Z_{i}^{0}\!=\!Z_{i}^{1}\!=\!y_{i}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:i-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:i-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:i-1}\right]\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}\\
\text{s.t. }y_{1:k}\in S_{k}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\Pr\!\left[Z_{k}^{0}\!=\!Z_{k}^{1}\!=\!y_{k}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:k-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:k-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:k-1}\right].

Conditioning on
Z_{1:k-1}^{0}=Z_{1:k-1}^{1}=y_{1:k-1}
, the pair
(Z_{k}^{0},Z_{k}^{1})
is the maximal coupling of
{Y_{i}^{0}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{0}=y_{1:i-1}^{0}}
and
{Y_{i}^{1}\mid Y_{1:i-1}^{1}=y_{1:i-1}^{1}}
. The innermost sum is taken over the common support of these conditional random variables. Thus, by Lemma
11.6
, this summation equals

1-\mathrm{TV}\left((Y_{k}^{0}\mid Y_{1:k-1}^{0}=y_{1:k-1}),(Y_{k}^{1}\mid Y_{1:k-1}^{1}=y_{1:k-1})\right).

Hence, by the first assumption,

(6)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}\\
\text{s.t. }y_{1:k}\in S_{k}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\Pr\!\left[Z_{k}^{0}\!=\!Z_{k}^{1}\!=\!y_{k}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:k-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:k-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:k-1}\right]\geq 1-\Delta_{k}(y_{1:k-1})

By the second assumption, we know

1-\Delta_{k}(y_{1:k-1})\geq\frac{1-\gamma}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}(1-\Delta_{i}(y_{1:i-1}))}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}]

\displaystyle\geq(1-\gamma)\sum_{y_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\frac{\Pr\!\left[Z_{i}^{0}\!=\!Z_{i}^{1}\!=\!y_{i}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:i-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:i-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:i-1}\right]}{1-\Delta_{i}(y_{1:i-1})}

\displaystyle=(1-\gamma)\sum_{y_{1:k-2}\in S_{k-2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}\frac{\Pr\!\left[Z_{i}^{0}\!=\!Z_{i}^{1}\!=\!y_{i}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:i-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:i-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:i-1}\right]}{1-\Delta_{i}(y_{1:i-1})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{k-1}\in\mathcal{Y}\\
\text{s.t. }y_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\frac{\Pr\!\left[Z_{k-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{k-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{k-1}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:k-2}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:k-2}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:k-1}\right]}{1-\Delta_{k-1}(y_{1:k-2})}.

The term
\Delta_{k-1}(y_{1:k-2})
is independent of
y_{k-1}
. Moreover, by an argument identical to the one for Inequality
6
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{k-1}\in\mathcal{Y}\\
\text{s.t. }y_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\Pr\!\left[Z_{k-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{k-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{k-1}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:k-2}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:k-2}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:k-1}\right]\geq 1-\Delta_{k-1}(y_{1:k-2}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}]\geq(1-\gamma)\sum_{y_{1:k-2}\in S_{k-2}}\prod_{i=1}^{k-2}\frac{\Pr\!\left[Z_{i}^{0}\!=\!Z_{i}^{1}\!=\!y_{i}\,\middle|\,Z_{1:i-1}^{0}\!=\!Z_{1:i-1}^{1}\!=\!y_{1:i-1}\right]}{1-\Delta_{i}(y_{1:i-1})}

A backward iteration with the same proof shows that
\Pr[Z^{0}=Z^{1}]\geq 1-\gamma
. Consequently,
\Pr[Z^{0}\neq Z^{1}]\leq\gamma
, and by Lemma
11.7
,
\mathrm{TV}(Y^{0},Y^{1})\leq\gamma
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.9
.

By Corollary
11.2
and the definition of concurrent composition using the continual mechanism
ExtConComp
, it suffices if we show that for every deterministic adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the random variables
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}})
and
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}})
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable. Fix a deterministic adversary
\mathcal{A}
.

Since
\mathcal{A}
is deterministic, its sequence of random coins is empty (or predetermined), and its
i
-th query message to the verifier
\mathcal{V}[f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}]
can be determined by the first
i-1
responses from the verifier. Therefore, there exists a post-processing function
g
such that for every
b\in\{0,1\}
,
g(V^{b})
is equivalently distributed as
\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}})
, where
V^{b}
is the (finite) sequence of responses
V^{b}_{i}
sent by
\mathcal{V}[f^{\delta}_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
to
\mathcal{A}
. By the (non-interactive) post-processing lemma, it suffices to prove that the random variables
V^{0}
and
V^{1}
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable.

For each
b\in\{0,1\}
, define
W^{b}=(W_{1}^{b},W_{2}^{b},\dots)
as the sequence
V^{b}
padded with an infinite number of
\$
symbols. For short, we denote the subsequences
(W_{1}^{b},\dots,W_{i}^{b})
and
(W_{i}^{b},W_{i+1}^{b},\dots)
by
W_{1:i}^{b}
and
W_{i:}^{b}
, respectively. We also define
S_{i}^{b}=\mathrm{supp}(W_{1:i}^{b})
. By Lemma
11.4
, to show
V^{0}
and
V^{1}
are
(0,\delta)
-indistinguishable, we must prove
\mathrm{TV}(V^{0},V^{1})\leq\delta
. Moreover, by Lemma
11.5
, to prove
\mathrm{TV}(V^{0},V^{1})\leq\delta
, it suffices to prove for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:k}^{0},W_{1:k}^{1})\leq\delta
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In the rest of the proof, we will show that
\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:k}^{0},W_{1:k}^{1})\leq\delta
.

As
\mathcal{A}
is deterministic, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
w_{1:i-1}=(w_{1},\dots,w_{i-1})\in S_{i-1}^{b}
, the random variable
W_{i}^{b}
conditioned on
W_{1:i-1}=w_{1:i-1}
is distributed as follows:

•

If
w_{i-1}=\$
(i.e., the communication has halted before) or the design of
\mathcal{A}
is such that it halts the communication after receiving the
(i-1)
-th response
w_{i-1}
given the history
w_{1:i-1}
, then
W_{1:i-1}=\$
with probability
1
.

•

Otherwise,
W_{1:i-1}
is equivalently distributed to
\mathrm{RR}_{0,\delta_{i}^{\prime}}(b)
, where
\delta_{i}^{\prime}=\delta_{i}^{\prime}(w_{1:i-1},\mathcal{A})
is determined by the history
w_{1:i-1}
and the design of
\mathcal{A}
.

Define
\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1})=0
in the former case and
\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1})=\delta_{i}^{\prime}(w_{1:i-1},\mathcal{A})
in the latter. By Lemmas
11.4
and
2.4
, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and every
w_{1:i-1}\in S_{i-1}^{b}
,

\mathrm{TV}\left((W_{i}^{0}|W_{1:i-1}^{0}=w_{1:i-1}),(W_{i}^{1}|W_{1:i-1}^{1}=w_{1:i-1})\right)\leq\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1}).

Fix
w_{1:k-1}\in S_{k-1}^{0}
. Let
j
be the smallest index such that
w_{j}=\$
. If there is no such index, set
j=k
. By Definition
5.8
(iii),
1-\prod_{i=1}^{j}(1-\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1}))\leq\delta
for every
w_{1:i-1}\in S_{i-1}^{0}\cap S_{i-1}^{1}
. Moreover, by the definition of the functions
\Delta_{i}
, we have
\prod_{i=j+1}^{k}(1-\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1}))=1
. Therefore,
1-\prod_{i=1}^{k}(1-\Delta_{i}(w_{1:i-1}))\leq\delta
. Thus, by Lemma
11.8
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}(W_{1:k}^{0},W_{1:k}^{1})\leq\delta,

finishing the proof.
∎

12.
Concurrent Filter Composition

In the parallel composition of continual mechanisms, the adversary sends pairs of identical queries to all but
k
mechanisms with predetermined privacy parameters. In this section, we study a concurrent composition where the adversary can ask pairs of non-identical queries from all created mechanisms and choose the privacy parameters of all mechanisms adaptively. To limit the adversary’s power in this more general setting, we impose restrictions on the sequence of adaptively chosen privacy parameters. These restrictions are captured using a class of functions known as
filters
:

Definition 12.1.

A
filter
is a function
\mathrm{Filt}:\left([0,\infty)\times[0,1]\right)^{*}\to\{\top,\bot\}
that takes a finite sequence of privacy parameters
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
and returns either
\top
or
\bot
. The output
\bot
indicates that the privacy budget has been exhausted, and the interaction must be halted.

Given a filter
\mathrm{Filt}
, we define a verification function
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
as follows and refer to the
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of CMs. The verification function
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
verifies the validity of message formats, extracts the privacy parameters from the creation queries, and applies
\mathrm{Filt}
on them.

Definition 12.2.

Let
\mathrm{Filt}
be a filter. For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every input sequence of messages
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
, the verification function
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
returns
\top
if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(i)

The sequence
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
is suitable.

(ii)

Let
\ell
denote the number of pairs of identical creation queries in the sequence
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
, and let
\sigma=\left((\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})\right)_{j=1}^{\ell}
be the corresponding sequence of privacy parameters for the mechanisms created in those pairs. It must hold that
\mathrm{Filt}(\sigma)=\top
.

Rogers et al.
(
29
)
studied the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of NIMs for the first time. More recently, Haney et al.
(
15
)
showed that if the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of NIMs is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then the same guarantee extends to the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of IMs. We further generalize this result to the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of CMs. To state our result, we need to define the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of randomized response mechanisms:

Definition 12.3 (
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{RR}}
).

Let
\mathrm{Filt}
be a filter. For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every input sequence of messages
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
, the function
