f^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{RR}}
returns
\top
if and only if all of the following conditions hold:

(i)

f^{\mathrm{Filt}}(m_{1},\dots,m_{t})=\top
.

(ii)

All mechanisms created in
m_{1},\dots,m_{t}
are randomized response mechanisms.

We refer to the
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{RR}}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of randomized response mechanisms.

Theorem 12.4.

For every filter
\mathrm{Filt}
, if the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of randomized response mechanisms is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, then the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of CMs is also
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Proof.

Let
f=f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
and
f_{\mathrm{RR}}=f^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{RR}}
. This proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
4.10
, except for the following modifications: First, the right mechanism of the IPM
\mathcal{P}
is
\mathcal{V}[f_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
instead of
{\textrm{Comp}}[\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}]\left((b)_{i=1}^{k}\right)
. Second, the variable
\sigma_{1}
is set differently. In Theorem
4.10
,
\mathcal{P}
initially interacted with its right mechanism,
{\textrm{Comp}}[\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}]\left((b)_{i=1}^{k}\right)
, and set
\sigma_{1}
to its output sequence
(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
, without ever changing it. Here,
\mathcal{P}
interacts with its right mechanism,
\mathcal{V}[f_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, each time it receives a pair of creation queries as a left message and appends a randomized response
r_{i}
to
\sigma_{1}
.

Specifically, as before, the state of
\mathcal{P}
consists of two sequences
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
, both initialized empty. Upon receiving a left message of the form
(\mathcal{M}_{i},s_{i},\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i},f_{i})^{2}
,
\mathcal{P}
takes the following steps: First,
\mathcal{P}
appends the pair
(\mathcal{T}_{i},t_{i})
to the sequence
\sigma_{2}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is the IPM corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{i}
from Lemma
4.7
and
t_{i}
is its initial state. Then, it sends the message
(\beta_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}},\beta_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}})
to its right mechanism
\mathcal{V}[f_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
, where
\beta_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}}
is the creation query corresponding to
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}}
. Upon receiving the acknowledgment response
\top
from
\mathcal{V}[f_{\mathrm{RR}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
,
\mathcal{P}
discards it and sends the message
\left((0,i),(1,i)\right)
to its right mechanism again. This message will be transformed by
\mathcal{I}(b)
to
(b,i)
. Receiving
(b,i)
, the mechanism
ExtConComp
sends
b
to
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}}
and returns its response
r_{i}
, which will be forwarded unchanged to
\mathcal{P}
.
\mathcal{P}
then appends
r_{i}
to
\sigma_{1}
. Finally,
\mathcal{P}
returns the acknowledgment message
\top
to its left mechanism.

Upon receiving a left message
\left((q_{0},i),(q_{1},i)\right)
, exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem
4.10
,
\mathcal{P}
uses
r_{i}
and
(\mathcal{T}_{i},t_{i})
to generate a left response.
To ensure that the privacy argument from Theorem
4.10
still holds under this modified construction, we must verify that
\mathcal{P}
can always provide each
\mathcal{T}_{i}
with a fresh output of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}}(b)
. This follows directly from the definitions of
f
and
f_{\mathrm{RR}}
and the design of
\mathcal{P}
. Specifically, the verifier
\mathcal{V}[f_{\mathrm{RR}}]
never halts the communication and always returns the requested randomized response.
∎

13.
Rényi DP

In this section, we show concurrent composition theorems for Rényi differential privacy (RDP), defined as follows.

Definition 13.1 (Rényi Divergence).

For
\alpha>1
, the
\alpha
-Rényi divergence between two distributions
P
and
Q
is defined as

D_{\alpha}(P\|Q)=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb{E}_{y\sim Q}\left[\left(\frac{P(y)}{Q(y)}\right)^{\alpha}\right].

By taking the limit as
\alpha\to\infty
, we obtain the
\infty
-Rényi divergence,

D_{\infty}(P\|Q)=\lim_{\alpha\to\infty}D_{\alpha}(P\|Q)=\log\max_{y}\frac{P(y)}{Q(y)}.

Definition 13.2 (Rényi Differential Privacy (RDP)
(
25
)
).

For
1<\alpha\leq\infty
and
\epsilon\geq 0
, a NIM
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is said to be
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-Rényi differentially private (or
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP) w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
if for all neighboring datasets
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}
, the
\alpha
-Rényi divergence between the output distributions satisfies

D_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{M}(x_{0}),\mathcal{M}(x_{1})\right)\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{ and }\qquad D_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{M}(x_{1}),\mathcal{M}(x_{0})\right)\leq\epsilon.

Note that the above definition includes pure DP as a special case. Lyu
(
24
)
and Vadhan et al.
(
34
)
extend this definition to IMs, which we further generalize to CMs:

Definition 13.3 (RDP for CMs).

For
1<\alpha\leq\infty
and
\epsilon\geq 0
, a continual mechanism
\mathcal{M}
is said to be
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-Rényi differentially private (or
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP) w.r.t. a verification function
f
against adaptive adversaries if for every adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the random variables
V^{0}=\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
and
V^{1}=\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
satisfy

D_{\alpha}(V^{0},V^{1})\leq\epsilon\qquad\text{ and }\qquad D_{\alpha}(V^{1},V^{0})\leq\epsilon.

Recall the concurrent composition of
k
CMs with fixed privacy parameters
\{(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
, which was formally defined via a verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
in Definition
4.11
. This verification function ensures that every creation query
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
is truthful, meaning that the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is
(\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f_{j}^{\prime}
, and the multiset of parameters
(\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})
for all created mechanisms forms a sub-multiset of the predetermined parameters
\{(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
.

For RDP, creation queries are of the form
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
, and the corresponding concurrent composition verification function must ensure that each
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is
(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime})
-RDP with respect to
f_{j}^{\prime}
. Fixing
\alpha
and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
, this verification function must check that all created mechanisms satisfy
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}=\alpha
, and that the multiset of parameters
\epsilon_{j}^{\prime}
is a sub-multiset of the prefix multiset
\{\epsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
. With these modifications to Definition
4.11
, we denote the resulting verification function by
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
and refer to the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the
\alpha
-RDP concurrent composition
of CMs with parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
.

Lyu
(
24
)
analyzed the concurrent composition of
k
fixed IMs
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
, where each
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is
(\alpha,\epsilon_{i})
-RDP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
. The analysis considers an adversary that concurrently interacts with
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
, initialized with states (datasets)
s_{1},\dots,s_{k}
, and compares this interaction to one where the mechanisms are initialized with neighboring states
s_{1}^{\prime},\dots,s_{k}^{\prime}
, such that
s_{i}\sim_{i}s_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i\in[k]
. Lyu shows that the
\alpha
-Rényi divergence between the resulting adversarial view distributions is at most
\epsilon=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i}
. This result was originally proved to hold assuming that the number of queries made by the adversary is bounded by a fixed integer
T
. However, Haney et al.
(
15
)
later demonstrated that this assumption can be removed. (See the application of the result of Van Erven and Harremoës
(
35
)
in their paper.)

We can replace each IM
\mathcal{M}_{i}
by a
universal interactive mechanism

\mathcal{UM}_{i}
whose initial state space is
\{0,1\}
. Given an initial state
b\in\{0,1\}
, the mechanism
\mathcal{UM}_{i}
receives as its first message a creation query of the form
(\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime},s_{i}^{\prime},\alpha,\epsilon_{i},f_{i})
and executes
\mathcal{V}[f_{i}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}(s_{i}^{\prime})
from that point on. If the initial message is malformed or if
\mathcal{M}_{i}^{\prime}
does not satisfy
(\alpha,\epsilon_{i})
-RDP w.r.t.
f_{i}
, then
\mathcal{UM}_{i}
immediately halts the interaction. It is straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{UM}_{i}
is
(\alpha,\epsilon_{i})
-RDP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim_{01}
defined by
0\sim_{01}1
. By construction, the concurrent composition of the universal mechanisms
\mathcal{UM}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{UM}_{k}
is equivalent to the
\alpha
-RDP concurrent composition of CMs with parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
. Hence, the result of Lyu
(
24
)
for IMs implies the following.

Corollary 13.4.

For
1<\alpha\leq\infty
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\geq 0
, the
\alpha
-RDP concurrent composition of CMs with parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
is
(\alpha,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i})
-RDP.

Previously, the concurrent
parallel
composition of CMs was defined via the verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty}
in Definition
5.1
. Analogously, we define the verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
for RDP with the following modifications: (i) Creation queries are of the form
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
rather than
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
. (ii) The verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
ensures that each
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is
(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime})
-RDP w.r.t.
f_{j}^{\prime}
, and that all
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}
equal the fixed
\alpha
. We refer to the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,RDP,\alpha}
-concurrent composition as
the
\alpha
-RDP concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of CMs with privacy parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
. The following theorem shows that this concurrent composition is not necessarily Rényi private:

Theorem 13.5.

For every
1<\alpha<\infty
and
\epsilon\geq 0
, the
f^{\epsilon}_{\infty,RDP,\alpha}
-concurrent composition is not
(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime})
-DP for every
\epsilon^{\prime}\geq 0
.

Proof.

Recall the proof of Theorem
5.2
, in which an adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}
repeatedly created instances of a
(0,\delta)
-DP CM, denoted
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
, with a pair of zeros as its first input pair until one mechanism returned
\bot
as response. At that point,
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}
sent the input pair
(0,1)
to the last mechanism, and the mechanism responded with the bit held by the identifier, thereby revealing it. In that construction,
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
was
(0,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. a verification function
g
that was defined to accept only message sequences of length at most two, where each message was a pair of bits and at most one pair included different bits.

This proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
5.2
, except that the adversary
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
creates instances of a mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
that is
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP w.r.t. the (same) verification function
g
, and instead of a full disclosure of the identifier’s bit, we will show that the
f^{\epsilon}_{\infty,RDP,\alpha}
-concurrent composition fails to satisfy
(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime})
-DP.

Definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
:

Like
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
, the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
receives single-bit inputs and outputs a response in
\{0,1,\top,\bot\}
. Upon receiving its first input bit,
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
outputs
\bot
with probability

p=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2},\epsilon\cdot(\alpha-1)\cdot 6^{-\alpha}\cdot 2^{-\epsilon^{\prime}\cdot(\alpha-1)}\right\},

and outputs
\top
otherwise.
If the first response was
\top
, then on its second input,
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
again outputs
\bot
with probability
p
and
\top
otherwise. However, if the first response was
\bot
, then on its next input it outputs a bit correlated with the input bit. In the
(0,\delta)
-DP construction,
\mathcal{M}_{\delta}
revealed the input bit exactly, resulting in a full disclosure of the private bit. In contrast,
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
outputs the true input bit with probability

q=1-\frac{3}{4}2^{-2-\epsilon^{\prime}\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}},

and outputs the opposite bit otherwise. Note that
q\geq 1-\frac{3}{4}2^{-2}>\frac{3}{4}
and
q\leq 1
.

\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
is
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP w.r.t.
g
:

By the definition of the verification function
g
, the mechanism
\mathcal{V}[g]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
interacts with an adversary at most twice and accepts a pair of bits each time, where at most one pair contains differing bits. By construction of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
, the
\alpha
-Rényi divergence between the adversary’s views is maximized when the adversary first sends identical bits, and when observing the response
\bot
, it sends a pair of differing bits as the second query. Let
\mathcal{A}
denote such an adversary. For
b\in\{0,1\}
, we denote the view of
\mathcal{A}
interacting with
\mathcal{V}[g]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
by
V_{\mathcal{A}}^{b}
.

For every view
v
where the first response of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
is
\top
, the probabilities
\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}=v]
and
\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}=v]
are equal; hence,

\left(\frac{\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}=v]}{\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}=v]}\right)^{\alpha}=1.

For every view
v
whose first response is
\bot
, the term
\bigl(\frac{\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}=v]}{\Pr[V_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}=v]}\bigr)^{\alpha}
equals
(\frac{q}{1-q})^{\alpha}
if the second response is
0
, and
(\frac{1-q}{q})^{\alpha}
if the second response is
1
. Since
q\in[3/4,1]
, we have
\frac{1-q}{q}\leq\frac{1}{1-q}
. Thus, both
(\frac{q}{1-q})^{\alpha}
and
(\frac{1-q}{q})^{\alpha}
are upper bounded by
(\frac{1}{1-q})^{\alpha}
. The probability that the first response in the random variable in
V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}
equals
\bot
is
p
. Thus, for the worst-case adversary
\mathcal{A}
we have

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0}\|V_{\mathcal{A}}^{1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left((1-p)\cdot 1+p\cdot\left(\frac{1}{1-q}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1+p\cdot\left(\frac{4}{3}2^{2+\epsilon^{\prime}\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1+p\cdot 6^{\alpha}\cdot 2^{\epsilon^{\prime}(\alpha-1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(1+\epsilon\cdot(\alpha-1)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon

In the last inequality, we use the fact that
\log(1+x)\leq x
for every
x\geq 0
.
By symmetry,
D_{\alpha}(V_{\mathcal{A}}^{1}\|V_{\mathcal{A}}^{0})\leq\epsilon
as well, and therefore
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
satisfies
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP w.r.t.
g
.

The
f^{\epsilon}_{\infty,\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition is not
(\alpha,\epsilon^{\prime})
-DP:

For
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
V^{b}
denote the view of
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
interacting with

\mathcal{V}[f^{\epsilon}_{\infty,\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
.
We show that
D_{\alpha}(V^{0}\|V^{1})>\epsilon^{\prime}
.

By definition,
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
eventually receives a
\bot
response from an instance of
\mathcal{M}_{\alpha,\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}}
. It then sends
(0,1)
to that instance and receives an answer
a\in\{0,1\}
. For every view
v
where
a=0
,

\left(\frac{\Pr[V^{0}=v]}{\Pr[V^{1}=v]}\right)^{\alpha}=\left(\frac{q}{1-q}\right)^{\alpha},

and for every view
v
with
a=1
,

\left(\frac{\Pr[V^{0}=v]}{\Pr[V^{1}=v]}\right)^{\alpha}=\left(\frac{1-q}{q}\right)^{\alpha}.

When sampling
v
from
V^{0}
, the event
a=0
occurs with probability
q
. Hence,

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}(V^{0}\|V^{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(q\cdot\left(\frac{q}{1-q}\right)^{\alpha}+(1-q)\cdot\left(\frac{q}{q}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(q\left(\frac{q}{1-q}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(\frac{3}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{4(1-q)}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(\frac{3}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{3}{4}\cdot 2^{-2-\epsilon^{\prime}\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}}}\right)^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\left(\frac{3}{4}\cdot 2^{2+\epsilon^{\prime}(\alpha-1)}\right)

\displaystyle>\epsilon^{\prime}.

∎

As in Section
5.3
, to overcome this lower bound, we modify the verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
to only create mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
whose associated verification function
f_{j}^{\prime}
is first-pair consistent (see Definition
5.12
). Let
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC},\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
denote this verification function.

Theorem 13.6.

For
\alpha>1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\geq 0
, the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC},\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs is
(\alpha,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i})
-RDP.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the proof of Theorem
5.14
, with a few modifications in the construction of
\mathcal{P}
. Recall that in the proof of Theorem
5.14
, receiving the
j
-th pair of identical creation queries
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
, the IPM
\mathcal{P}
temporarily stored the creation query until it received the first query pair for mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{j}
, i.e., a message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
. Omitting the implementation details, the idea was that
\mathcal{P}
instantiated either an IM
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
if
q_{0}=q_{1}
on the first query pair or an IPM
\mathcal{T}_{j}
otherwise, where
\mathcal{T}_{j}
was the IPM corresponding to
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
from Lemma
4.7
. This lemma constructed
\mathcal{T}_{j}
so that the IMs
\mathcal{T}_{j}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime}}(b)
and
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
are equivalent for every
b\in\{0,1\}
. We will call
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
a
type-1
CM in the former case and a
type-2
CM in the latter. Upon receiving a left message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
, the IPM
\mathcal{P}
forwarded
(q_{0},q_{1})
either to
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
(type 1) or to
\mathcal{T}_{j}
(type 2), and returned the response as a left message.

The IPM
\mathcal{T}_{j}
might want to interact with its right mechanism, requesting the outcome of
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime}}(b)
. To supply answers,
\mathcal{P}
initially interacted with
{\textrm{Comp}}(\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\ldots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}})
, stored its response
(r_{1},\dots,r_{k})
, and later used these
r_{i}
’s as right responses to the IPMs
\mathcal{T}_{j}
. This reduced the
f_{\infty,\mathit{FPC}}^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
-concurrent composition of CMs to the composition of the noninteractive mechanisms
\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
.

In this proof we make the following changes:

(1)

Creation queries now have the form
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
instead of
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
.

(2)

The right mechanism of
\mathcal{P}
is

\mathcal{V}[f^{\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}},

instead of
{\textrm{Comp}}(\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}},\dots,\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}})
.

(3)

Rather than instantiating
\mathcal{T}_{j}
via Lemma
4.7
when
q_{0}=q_{1}
on the first query pair,
\mathcal{P}
now forwards the pair of identical creation queries
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})^{2}
to its right mechanism and stores the index
i_{j}
indicating that
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
corresponds to the
i_{j}
-th mechanism created by the right mechanism of
\mathcal{P}
.

(4)

Upon receiving a left message of the form
\left((q_{0},j),(q_{1},j)\right)
, if
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is a type-2 CM, then
\mathcal{P}
forwards the message
\left((q_{0},i_{j}),(q_{1},i_{j})\right)
to its right mechanism and returns that response to the left. If
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is a type-1 CM, then as before
\mathcal{P}
forwards
(q_{0},q_{1})
to
\mathcal{V}[f_{j}^{\prime}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
and returns the resulting answer.

The exact same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5.14
shows that the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC},\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs satisfies the privacy guarantees of the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs. By Theorem
13.4
, the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs is
(\alpha,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i})
-RDP. Therefore, the
f^{\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{k}}_{\infty,\mathit{FPC},\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
-concurrent composition of CMs is also
(\alpha,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i})
-RDP, completing the proof.
∎

As discussed in Section
5.3.1
, IMs satisfy the first-pair consistent condition. Thus, by Theorem
13.6
, we have:

Corollary 13.7.

For
\alpha>1
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}\geq 0
, the
\alpha
-RDP concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of
IMs
with privacy parameters
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k}
is
(\alpha,\sum_{i=1}^{k}\epsilon_{i})
-RDP.

Finally, to define concurrent filter composition of RDP CMs, we modify Definition
12.1
so that a filter
\mathrm{Filt}
maps a finite sequence of nonnegative reals in
[0,\infty)^{*}
(instead of a sequence in
\left([0,\infty)\times[0,1]\right)^{*}
) to an element in
\{\top,\bot\}
. Fix
\alpha>1
. As before, we obtain an RDP version of the verification function
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
in Definition
12.2
by replacing creation queries to use RDP parameters, ensuring that each created mechanism with parameters
(\alpha_{j},\epsilon_{j})
is
(\alpha_{j},\epsilon_{j})
-RDP, and checking whether all
\alpha_{j}=\alpha
. We denote this verification function by
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha}
.

For parameters
\alpha>1
and
\epsilon\geq 0
, we define the filter
\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}
as follows. For every
t\in\mathbb{N}
and every
\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{t}\in[0,\infty)^{t}
,

\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}(\epsilon_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{t})=\begin{cases}\top,&\text{if }\sum_{i=1}^{t}\epsilon_{i}\leq\epsilon,\\[4.0pt]
\bot,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We refer to the
f^{\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the
concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}
-filter composition
of CMs.

Haney et al.
(
15
)
show that the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}
-filter composition of IMs is
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP. Following the same argument as for the concurrent composition of
k
fixed RDP CMs, this result extends to CMs by replacing each IM with the universal IM with the same privacy parameters:

Corollary 13.8.

For every
\alpha>1
and
\epsilon\geq 0
, the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}_{\mathrm{RDP},\alpha,\epsilon}
-filter composition of CMs is
(\alpha,\epsilon)
-RDP against adaptive adversaries.

14.
f
-DP

In this section, we show concurrent composition theorems for
f
-differential privacy (
f
-DP), which generalizes
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP.
f
-DP takes a statistical point of view and uses trade-off functions to measure how indistinguishable the output distributions of a mechanism are for two neighboring datasets.

Remark 14.1.

To avoid ambiguity, throughout this section we use the symbol
g
to denote verification functions and the symbol
f
to denote privacy functions.

Consider a NIM
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
and two neighboring datasets
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}
. Observing the outcome of
\mathcal{M}
, an analyst/adversary
\mathcal{A}
must decide whether the input dataset was
x_{0}
(null hypothesis
H_{0}
) or
x_{1}
(alternative hypothesis
H_{1}
). A
rejection rule

\phi:\mathcal{Y}\to\{0,1\}
is a function that takes the answer of
\mathcal{M}
as input and decides whether to reject the null hypothesis or not. Thus, for
b\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathcal{A}
chooses dataset
x_{b}
when
\phi
returns
b
. To quantify the indistinguishability between
\mathcal{M}(x_{0})
and
\mathcal{M}(x_{1})
,
f
-DP evaluates the error made by the analyst in selecting the correct hypothesis. Specifically, given a rejection rule
\phi
, the
type I error

\alpha_{\phi}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathcal{M}(x_{0}))]
is the probability of rejecting the null hypothesis
H_{0}
while
H_{0}
is true (i.e., guessing
x_{1}
when the dataset is
x_{0}
). Similarly, the
type II error

\beta_{\phi}=1-\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathcal{M}(x_{1}))]
is the probability of not rejecting
H_{0}
while
H_{1}
is true (i.e., guessing
x_{0}
when the dataset is
x_{1}
). Although an analyst could achieve zero type II error by always rejecting the null hypothesis, when an upper bound on the type I error is set, the minimum achievable type II error reflects the difficulty of distinguishing between
\mathcal{M}(x_{0})
and
\mathcal{M}(x_{1})
. The following function measures the optimal trade-off between the type I and type II errors.

Definition 14.2 (Trade-off Function).

Let
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
be two random variables on the same domain
\mathcal{Y}
. The trade-off function between
Y_{0}
and
Y_{1}
, denoted
T(Y_{0},Y_{1}):[0,1]\to[0,1]
, is defined as:

T(Y_{0},Y_{1})(\alpha)=\inf_{\phi}\{\beta_{\phi}:\alpha_{\phi}\leq\alpha\},

where the infimum is taken over all possible rejection rules. Given a function
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
, we write
T(Y_{0},Y_{1})\geq f
if
T(Y_{0},Y_{1})(\alpha)\geq f(\alpha)
for every
\alpha\in[0,1]
.

The (meta-)function
T
in Definition
14.2
is indeed a function that maps a pair of random variables to a trade-off function between these random variables.

Definition 14.3 (
f
-DP for NIMs
(
8
)
).

Let
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a function. A NIM
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is
f
-differentially private (or
f
-DP) w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
if for every two neighboring datasets
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}
,

T(\mathcal{M}(x_{0}),\mathcal{M}(x_{1}))\geq f,

where
T(\mathcal{M}(x_{0}),\mathcal{M}(x_{1}))
denotes the trade-off function defined in Definition
14.2
.

For
\epsilon\geq 0
and
0\leq\delta\leq 1
, define the function
f_{\epsilon,\delta}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
as
f_{\epsilon,\delta}(\alpha)=\max\{0,1-\delta-\exp(\epsilon)\alpha,\exp(-\epsilon)(1-\delta-\alpha)\}
for every
\alpha\in[0,1]
. Dong, Roth, and Su
(
8
)
show that
f_{\epsilon,\delta}
-DP is equivalent to
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. They also show that if a NIM
\mathcal{N}
satisfies
f
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
, where
f
is the trade-off function of two random variables
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
, then for every pair of neighboring datasets
x_{0}\sim x_{1}
, there exists a randomized post-processing function
Post
such that, for each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the distributions of
\mathcal{N}(x_{b})
and
\textsc{Post}(Z_{b})
are identical.

Vadhan and Zhang
(
34
)
prove an analogue of Lemma
4.7
for
f
-DP under an additional
finite communication
assumption, defined below.

Definition 14.4 (Finite Communication for IMs).

An IM
\mathcal{M}
is said to have
finite communication
if there exists a constant
c
such that both its query space
Q_{\mathcal{M}}
and answer space
A_{\mathcal{M}}
have cardinality at most
c
, and
\mathcal{M}
terminates after receiving more than
c
queries.

Lemma 14.5 (
(
34
)
).

Let
f
be a privacy function, and let
\mathcal{M}
be an IM that is
f
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim
and has finite communication. Then, for every pair of neighboring datasets
x_{0}\sim x_{1}
, there exists an IPM
\mathcal{P}
with a single initial state and a NIM
\mathcal{N}
taking a bit as input such that the IMs
\mathcal{P}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{N}(b)
and
\mathcal{M}(x_{b})
are equivalent for every
b\in\{0,1\}
, and the mechanism
\mathcal{N}
is
f
-DP w.r.t. the neighbor relation
\sim_{01}
defined by
0\sim_{01}1
.

We now extend the definition of
f
-DP from IMs to CMs:

Definition 14.6 (
f
-DP for CMs).

Let
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be a function. A continual mechanism
\mathcal{M}
is said to be
f
-differentially private
(or
f
-DP
) w.r.t. a verification function
g
against adaptive adversaries if, for every adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the random variables
V^{0}=\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(0)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
and
V^{1}=\mathrm{View}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{V}[f]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(1)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M})
satisfy

T(V^{0},V^{1})\geq f,

where
T(V^{0},V^{1})
denotes the trade-off function defined in Definition
14.2
.

In the DP setting, creation queries were of the form
(\mathcal{M},s,\epsilon,\delta,f)
, where
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is a CM with the initial state
s
that is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
f
. In the
f
-DP setting, creation queries are of the form
(\mathcal{M},s,f,g)
, where
\mathcal{M}
is
f
-DP w.r.t. the verification function
g
. We emphasize that the symbol
f
in this section denotes a
privacy function
, whereas
f
in the previous DP sections denoted a
verification function
.

Recall that the concurrent composition of
k
CMs with fixed privacy parameters
\{(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k}
was defined via a verification function
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}
in Definition
4.11
. For the
f
-DP setting, we modify this verification function to accommodate creation queries of the form
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime},g_{j}^{\prime})
, rather than
(\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime},s_{j}^{\prime},\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime},f_{j}^{\prime})
.
Specifically, the new verification function must ensure that each created mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
is
f_{j}^{\prime}
-DP w.r.t.
g_{j}^{\prime}
and has
finite communication
. Given privacy functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
, the verification function must verify that the multiset of the privacy functions
f_{j}^{\prime}
is a sub-multiset of the prefix functions
\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
. With these modifications to Definition
4.11
,
we denote the resulting verification function by
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}
and refer to the
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}
-concurrent composition of CMs as the
concurrent composition of CMs with privacy functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
.

An argument identical to that of Theorem
4.12
, with Lemma
14.5
being used instead of Lemma
2.16
implies:

Corollary 14.7.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f,f_{1},\dots,f_{k}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be functions. Suppose that for every
k
NIMs
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
, where each
\mathcal{N}_{i}
is
f_{i}
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
, the composition of
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
is
f
-DP. Then, the concurrent composition of CMs with privacy functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
is also
f
-DP.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the proof of Theorem
4.12
, with Lemma
14.5
applied instead of Lemma
2.16
. For completeness, we outline how the additional finite communication requirement of Lemma
14.5
is satisfied.

Recall that in Theorem
4.12
, the
i
-th created mechanism,
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, was
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP w.r.t. a verification function
f_{i}
, which we denote here by
g_{i}
to avoid confusion. The proof of that theorem showed that for each
b\in\{0,1\}
, the IMs
\mathcal{V}[f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
and
\mathcal{V}[f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}]\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{IECC}}(b)
are equivalent, where
{\textrm{IECC}}(b)
was an IM internally executing the mechanism
\mathcal{V}[g_{i}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{i}
for each created mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. The argument then replaced each instance
\mathcal{V}[g_{i}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{i}
by
\mathcal{T}_{i}\!\circ^{*}\!\mathrm{RR}_{\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i}}(b)
, where
\mathcal{T}_{i}
is the IPM given by Lemma
2.16
.

In the
f
-DP setting, by the definition of
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}
, each created mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is required to have
finite communication
. Hence, its query space
Q_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}
and answer space
A_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}
are both finite, and
\mathcal{M}_{i}
halts before a certain number of interactions. Thus, the mechanism
\mathcal{V}[g_{i}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{i}
also has a finite answer space and terminates before the same number of steps. Although the verifier
\mathcal{V}[g_{i}]
may, in principle, have an infinite query space, the outer verifier
\mathcal{V}[g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}]
in
\mathcal{V}[g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}]\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{IECC}}
ensures that all messages sent to
\mathcal{M}_{i}
are
g_{i}
-valid. Consequently, the set of admissible query messages to
\mathcal{V}[g_{i}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{M}_{i}
is restricted to
Q_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}\times Q_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}
, which is finite. Therefore, we can assume that this mechanism has finite communication, satisfying the condition of Lemma
14.5
, and the rest of the proof follows as the proof of Theorem
4.12
.
∎

Similar to the concurrent composition of
k
CMs with fixed privacy parameters, we define, for given privacy functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
, the verification function
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty}
as the
f
-DP analogue of
f^{\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1},\dots,\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k}}_{\infty}
from Definition
5.1
. Since
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP is a special case of
f
-DP, Theorem
5.2
immediately yields a counterexample: there exists a privacy function
f_{1}
and an adversary
\mathcal{A}
such that the views of
\mathcal{A}
when interacting with
\mathcal{V}[g^{f_{1}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty}]\!\circ^{*}\!\mathcal{I}(b)\!\circ^{*}\!{\textrm{ExtConComp}}
have disjoint supports for
b=0
and
b=1
. Equivalently, the
g^{f_{1}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty}
-concurrent composition of CMs is not private.

To overcome the lower bound, as in Section
5.3
, we can define the verification function
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
identical to
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty}
with an additional condition that checks whether the created mechanisms are first-pair consistent. A proof identical to the proof of Theorem
5.14
shows:

Corollary 14.8.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f,f_{1},\dots,f_{k}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be functions. Suppose that for every
k
NIMs
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
, where each
\mathcal{N}_{i}
is
f_{i}
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
, the composition of
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
is
f
-DP. Then, the
g^{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}_{\mathrm{FDP},\infty,\mathit{FPC}}
-concurrent composition of CMs is also
f
-DP.

We know that IMs satisfy the first-pair consistent condition. Define
the concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of IMs with privacy functions

f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
as in Section
5.3.1
. We have:

Corollary 14.9.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
f,f_{1},\dots,f_{k}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
be functions. Suppose that for every
k
NIMs
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
, where each
\mathcal{N}_{i}
is
f_{i}
-DP w.r.t. a neighbor relation
\sim_{i}
, the composition of
\mathcal{N}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{N}_{k}
is
f
-DP. Then, the concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of IMs with privacy functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{k}
is also
f
-DP.

Finally, we extend the definition of
concurrent filter composition of CMs
to the
f
-DP setting. To this end, we modify Definition
12.1
so that a filter
\mathrm{Filt}
maps a finite sequence of functions
f_{1},\dots,f_{t}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
to an element of
\{\top,\bot\}
. As before, we construct the
f
-DP version of the verification function
f^{\mathrm{Filt}}
in Definition
12.2
by replacing the DP parameters with privacy functions and ensuring that every created CM has finite communication. We denote this verification function by
g^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}
.

Corollary 14.10.

Let
\mathrm{Filt}
be a filter and
f:[0,1]\to[0,1]
a function. If the
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of NIMs is
f
-DP, then the
g^{\mathrm{Filt}}_{\mathrm{FDP}}
-concurrent composition of CMs is also
f
-DP. Equivalently, the concurrent
\mathrm{Filt}
-filter composition of CMs is
f
-DP.

Appendix B
Variants of Concurrent Composition

Here, we elaborate on the different types of concurrent composition that we study in this paper:

(1)
Concurrent Composition of Continual Mechanisms:
In the
concurrent composition of fixed continual mechanism
,
k
continual mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
are fixed at the beginning, and an adversary adaptively issues pairs of messages for them such that for each
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, the sequences formed by the first messages of all message pairs and the sequence formed by the second messages of all message pairs are neighboring. A secret bit
b\in\{0,1\}
determines whether each mechanism receives the first or second sequence from each pair. The adversary’s goal is to guess
b
using the answers of all
k
mechanisms. Theorem
4.10
, which is the formal statement of Theorem
1.2
above, analyzes the privacy of this composition.

A natural extension is the
concurrent composition of continual mechanism with fixed parameters
, where instead of fixing the mechanisms themselves,
k
privacy parameter pairs
(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
are fixed in advance. The adversary then adaptively selects mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
over time, ensuring that, for each
1\leq i\leq k
, the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP. The privacy guarantee for this variant is the same as Theorem
4.10
and is stated in Theorem
4.12
.

(2)
Concurrent
k
-Sparse Parallel Composition of Continual Mechanisms:
This composition generalizes the concurrent composition of continual mechanisms with
k
fixed parameters by permitting the creation of an
arbitrary
number of continual mechanisms.
As before,
k
privacy parameters
(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
are fixed at the beginning. The adversary adaptively creates a potentially unlimited number of
continual
mechanisms and issues pairs of messages to each mechanism such that for all but at most
k
of these mechanisms, each message pair consists of identical messages. Let
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
denote the exceptional mechanisms, which may receive differing message sequences. For each
1\leq i\leq k
, the mechanism
\mathcal{M}_{i}
must be
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
-DP, and the sequences of the first and second messages for
\mathcal{M}_{i}
must be neighboring. We note the choice of mechanisms
\mathcal{M}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{M}_{k}
is made adaptively over time. Specifically, the adversary decides whether to issue a pair of differing messages for a mechanism based on the previous answers of all mechanisms, including the outputs of that mechanism.

As in previous compositions, a secret bit
b
determines whether each mechanism receives the first or second message from each pair, and the adversary seeks to guess
b
based on the outputs.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{j}^{\prime}
denote the
j
-th created mechanism, and let
(\epsilon_{j}^{\prime},\delta_{j}^{\prime})
be its privacy parameter. Theorem
5.4
, informally stated in Theorem
1.5
, analyzes the privacy of the concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of continual mechanisms when
\sum\delta_{j}^{\prime}
is upper bounded by a predetermined value.
Corollary
5.5
, informally stated in Corollary
1.7
, sets this upper bound to
0
and provides privacy guarantees for the concurrent
k
-sparse parallel composition of purely differentially private continual mechanisms.

(3)
Concurrent
k
-Sparse Parallel Composition of Interactive Mechanisms:
In this composition, the adversary creates an unbounded number of
interactive
mechanisms over time, instead of continual ones. As before,
k
privacy parameter pairs
(\epsilon_{1},\delta_{1}),\dots,(\epsilon_{k},\delta_{k})
are fixed in advance. Each time the adversary creates an interactive mechanism, they also select a pair of neighboring datasets for it. Instead of message pairs, the adversary adaptively sends (single) queries to each mechanism. A secret bit
b
determines whether the mechanism is run on the first or second dataset of its respective pair. The adversary then tries to infer
b
from the responses of all mechanisms. Theorem
1.3
analyzes the privacy of this composition when all
\mathcal{M}_{i}
are
(\epsilon_{0},\delta_{0})
-DP. The more general case for mechanisms with possibly different
(\epsilon_{i},\delta_{i})
parameters is stated in Theorem
5.14
.
